Microscopic Derivation of Ginzburg–Landau Theory and the BCS Critical Temperature Shift in the Presence of Weak Macroscopic External Fields
Andreas Deuchert, Christian Hainzl, Marcel Maier (born Schaub)

Abstract
We consider the Bardeen–Cooper–Schrieffer (BCS) free energy functional with weak and macroscopic external electric and magnetic fields and derive the Ginzburg–Landau functional. We also provide an asymptotic formula for the BCS critical temperature as a function of the external fields. This extends our previous results in [ 16 ] for the constant magnetic field to general magnetic fields with a nonzero magnetic flux through the unit cell.

1
Introduction and Main Results

1.1
Introduction

Ginzburg–Landau (GL) theory has been introduced as the first macroscopic and phenomenlogical description of superconductivity in 1950
[
29
]
. The theory comprises a system of partial differential equations for a complex-valued function, the order parameter, and an effective magnetic field. Ginzburg–Landau theory has been highly influencial and investigated in numerous works, among which are
[
7
,
8
,
46
,
45
,
10
,
11
,
13
,
12
,
1
]
and references therein.

Bardeen–Cooper–Schrieffer (BCS) theory of superconductivity is the first commonly accepted and Nobel prize awarded microscopic theory of superconductivity
[
2
]
. As a major breakthrough, the theory features a pairing mechanism between the electrons below a certain critical temperature, which causes the electrical resistance in the system to drop to zero in the superconducting phase. This effect is due to an effective attraction between the electrons, which arises as a consequence of the phonon vibrations of the lattice ions in the superconductor.

One way to formulate BCS theory mathematically is via the BCS free energy functional or BCS functional for short. As Leggett pointed out in
[
38
]
, the BCS functional can be obtained from a full quantum mechanical description of the system by restricting attention to quasi-free states, see also
[
28
]
. Such states are determined by their one-particle density matrix and the Cooper pair wave function. The BCS functional has been studied intensively from a mathematical point of view in the absence of external fields in
[
32
,
20
,
34
,
35
,
26
,
4
,
23
,
15
]
and in the presence of external fields in
[
36
,
5
,
24
,
14
,
6
]
. The BCS gap equation arises as the Euler–Lagrange equation of the BCS functional and its solution is used to compute the spectral gap of an effective Hamiltonian, which is open in the superconducting phase. BCS theory from the point of view of its gap equation is studied in
[
43
,
3
,
50
,
51
,
41
,
52
]
.

The present article continues a series of works, in which the
macroscopic
GL theory is derived from the
microscopic
BCS theory in a regime close to the critical temperature and for weak external fields. This endeavor has been initiated by Gor’kov in 1959
[
30
]
. The first mathematically rigorous derivation of the GL functional from the BCS functional has been provided by Frank, Hainzl, Seiringer, and Solovej for periodic external electric and magnetic fields in 2012 in
[
21
]
. An important assumption of this work is that the flux of the external magnetic field through the unit cell of periodicity of the system vanishes. This excludes for example a homogeneous magnetic field. The techniques from this GL derivation have been further developed in
[
22
]
to compute the BCS critical temperature shift caused by the external fields. The first important step towards overcoming the zero magnetic flux restriction in
[
21
,
19
]
has been made by Frank, Hainzl, and Langmann, who considered in
[
19
]
the problem of computing the BCS critical temperature shift for systems exposed to a homogeneous magnetic field within the framework of linearized BCS theory. Recently, the derivation of the GL functional and the computation of BCS critical temperature shift (for the full nonlinear model) could be extended to the case of a constant magnetic field by Deuchert, Hainzl, and Maier in
[
16
]
. The goal of the present work is to further extend the results in
[
16
]
to the case of general external magnetic fields with an arbitrary flux through the unit cell.

GL theory arises from BCS theory when the temperature is sufficiently close to the critical temperature and when the external fields are weak and slowly varying. More precisely, if
0<h\ll 1
denotes the ratio between the microscopic and the macroscopic length scale, then the external electric field
W
and the magnetic vector potential
\mathbf{A}
are given by
h^{2}W(hx)
and
h\mathbf{A}(hx)
, respectively. Furthermore, the temperature regime is such that
T-{T_{\mathrm{c}}}=-{T_{\mathrm{c}}}Dh^{2}
for some constant
D>0
, where
{T_{\mathrm{c}}}
is the critical temperature in absence of external fields. When this scaling is in effect, it is shown in
[
21
]
and
[
16
]
that the Cooper pair wave function
\alpha(x,y)
is given by

\displaystyle\alpha(x,y)=h\,\alpha_{*}(x-y)\,\psi\left(\frac{h(x+y)}{2}\right)

(1.1)

to leading order in
h
. Here,
\alpha_{*}
is the microscopic Cooper pair wave function in the absence of external fields and
\psi
is the GL order parameter.

Moreover, the influence of the external fields causes a shift in the critical temperature of the BCS model, which is described by linearized GL theory in the same scaling regime. More precisely, it has been shown in
[
22
,
19
]
, and
[
16
]
that the critical temperature shift in BCS theory is given by

\displaystyle{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(h)={T_{\mathrm{c}}}(1-{D_{\mathrm{c}}}h^{2})

(1.2)

to leading order, where
{D_{\mathrm{c}}}
denotes a critical parameter that can be computed using linearized GL theory.

The present work is an extension of the paper
[
16
]
, where the case of a constant magnetic field was considered. In this article, we incorporate periodic electric fields
W
and general vector potentials
\mathbf{A}
that give rise to periodic magnetic fields. This, in particular, generalizes the results in
[
21
,
22
]
to the case of general external magnetic fields with non-zero flux through the unit cell. We show that within the scaling introduced above, the Ginzburg–Landau energy arises as leading order correction on the order
h^{4}
. Furthermore, we show that the Cooper pair wave function admits the leading order term (
1.1
) and that the critical temperature shift is given by (
1.2
) to leading order. The main technical novelty of this article is a further development of the phase approximation method, which has been pioneered in the framework of BCS theory for the case of the constant magnetic field in
[
19
]
and
[
16
]
. It allows us to compute the BCS energy of a class of trial states (Gibbs states) in a controlled way. This trial state analysis is later used in the proofs of the upper and of the lower bound for the BCS free energy. The proof of our lower bound additionally uses a priori bounds for certain low-energy BCS states that include the magnetic field and have been established in
[
16
]
.

1.2
Gauge-periodic samples

Our objective is to study a system of three-dimensional fermionic particles that is subject to weak and slowly varying external electromagnetic fields within the framework of BCS theory. Let us define the magnetic field
\mathbf{B}\coloneqq h^{2}e_{3}
. It can be written in terms of the vector potential
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}\wedge x
, where
x\wedge y
denotes the cross product of two vectors
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, as
\mathbf{B}=\curl\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}
. To the vector potential
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}
we associate the magnetic translations

\displaystyle T(v)f(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(v\wedge x)}f(x+v),

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}^{3},

(1.3)

which commute with the magnetic momentum operator
-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}
. The family
\{T(v)\}_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}
satisfies
T(v+w)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(v\wedge w)}T(v)T(w)
and is therefore a unitary representation of the Heisenberg group. We assume that our system is periodic with respect to the Bravais lattice
\Lambda_{h}\coloneqq\sqrt{2\pi}\,h^{-1}\,\mathbb{Z}^{3}
with fundamental cell

\displaystyle Q_{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigl[0,\sqrt{2\pi}\,h^{-1}\bigr]^{3}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}.

(1.4)

Let
b_{i}=\sqrt{2\pi}\,h^{-1}\,e_{i}
denote the basis vectors that span
\Lambda_{h}
. The magnetic flux through the face of the unit cell spanned spanned by
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
equals
2\pi
, and hence the abelian subgroup
\{T(\lambda)\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{h}}
is a unitary representation of the lattice group.

Our system is subject to an external electric field
W_{h}(x)=h^{2}W(hx)
with a fixed function
W\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, as well as a magnetic field defined in terms of the vector potential
\mathbf{A}_{h}(x)=h\mathbf{A}(hx)
, which admits the form
\mathbf{A}\coloneqq\mathbf{A}_{e_{3}}+A
with
A\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\mathbf{A}_{e_{3}}
as defined above. We assume that
A
and
W
are periodic with respect to
\Lambda_{1}
. The flux of the magnetic field
\curl A_{h}
through all faces of the unit cell
Q_{h}
vanishes because
A_{h}
is a periodic function. Accordingly, the magnetic field
\curl\mathbf{A}_{h}
has the same fluxes through the faces of the unit cell as
\mathbf{B}
.

The above representation of
\mathbf{A}_{h}
is general in the sense that any periodic magnetic field field
B(x)
that satisfies the Maxwell equation
\divv B=0
can be written as the curl of a vector potential
A_{B}
of the form
A_{B}(x)=\frac{1}{2}b\wedge x+A_{\mathrm{per}}(x)
, where
b
denotes the vector with components given by the average magnetic flux of
B
through the faces of
Q_{h}
and
A_{\mathrm{per}}
is a periodic vector potential. For more information concerning this decomposition we refer to
[
40
, Chapter 4]
. For a treatment of the two-dimensional case, see
[
49
]
.

1.3
The BCS functional

In BCS theory a state is conveniently described by its generalized one-particle density matrix, that is, by a self-adjoint operator
\Gamma
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, which obeys
0\leqslant\Gamma\leqslant 1
and is of the form

\displaystyle\Gamma=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma&\alpha\\
\overline{\alpha}&1-\overline{\gamma}\end{pmatrix}.

(1.5)

Here,
\overline{\alpha}
denotes the operator
\alpha
with the complex conjugate integral kernel in the position space representation. Since
\Gamma
is self-adjoint we know that
\gamma
is self-adjoint and that
\alpha
is symmetric in the sense that its integral kernel satisfies
\alpha(x,y)=\alpha(y,x)
. This symmetry is related to the fact that we exclude spin degrees of freedom from our description and assume that all Cooper pairs are in a spin singlet state. The condition
0\leqslant\Gamma\leqslant 1
implies that the one-particle density matrix
\gamma
satisfies
0\leqslant\gamma\leqslant 1
and that
\alpha
and
\gamma
are related through the inequality

\displaystyle\alpha\alpha^{*}\leqslant\gamma(1-\gamma).

(1.6)

Let us define the magnetic translations
\mathbf{T}(\lambda)
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
by

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}(v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}T(v)&0\\
0&\overline{T(v)}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

We say that a BCS state
\Gamma
is
gauge-periodic
provided
\mathbf{T}(\lambda)\,\Gamma\,\mathbf{T}(\lambda)^{*}=\Gamma
holds for any
\lambda\in\Lambda_{h}
. This implies the relations
T(\lambda)\,\gamma\,T(\lambda)^{*}=\gamma
and
T(\lambda)\,\alpha\,\overline{T(\lambda)}^{*}=\alpha
, or, in terms of integral kernels,

\displaystyle\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(\lambda\wedge(x-y))}\;\gamma(x+\lambda,y+\lambda),

\displaystyle\alpha(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(\lambda\wedge(x+y))}\;\alpha(x+\lambda,y+\lambda),

\displaystyle\lambda\in\Lambda_{h}.

(1.7)

We further say that a gauge-periodic BCS state
\Gamma
is
admissible
if

\displaystyle\Tr\bigl[\gamma+(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}})^{2}\gamma\bigr]<\infty

(1.8)

holds. Here
\Tr[\mathcal{R}]
denotes the trace per unit volume of an operator
\mathcal{R}
defined by

\displaystyle\Tr[\mathcal{R}]

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{|Q_{h}|}\Tr_{L^{2}(Q_{h})}[\chi\mathcal{R}\chi],

(1.9)

where
\chi
denotes the characteristic function of the cube
Q_{h}
in (
1.4
) and
\Tr_{L^{2}(Q_{h})}[\cdot]
is the usual trace over an operator on
L^{2}(Q_{h})
. By the condition in (
1.8
), we mean that
\chi\gamma\chi
and
\chi(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}})^{2}\gamma\chi
are trace-class operators.
Eqs. (
1.6
), (
1.8
), and the same inequality with
\gamma
replaced by
\overline{\gamma}
imply that
\alpha
,
(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}})\alpha
, and
(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}})\overline{\alpha}
are locally Hilbert–Schmidt. We will rephrase this property as a notion of
H^{1}
-regularity for the kernel of
\alpha
in Section
2
below.

Let
\Gamma
be an admissible BCS state. We define the Bardeen–Cooper–Schrieffer free energy functional, or BCS functional for short, at temperature
T\geqslant 0
by the formula

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Tr\bigl[\bigl((-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{h})^{2}-\mu+W_{h}\bigr)\gamma\bigr]-T\,S(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\hskip 120.0pt-\frac{1}{|Q_{h}|}\int_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;V(r)\,|\alpha(X,r)|^{2},

(1.10)

where
S(\Gamma)=-\Tr[\Gamma\ln(\Gamma)]
denotes the von Neumann entropy per unit volume and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
is a chemical potential. The interaction energy is written in terms of the center-of-mass and relative coordinates
X=\frac{x+y}{2}
and
r=x-y
. Throughout this paper, we write, by a slight abuse of notation,
\alpha(x,y)\equiv\alpha(X,r)
. That is, we use the same symbol for the function depending on the original coordinates and for the one depending on
X
and
r
.

The natural space for the interaction potential guaranteeing that the BCS functional is bounded from below is
V\in L^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})+L_{\varepsilon}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, that is, the set of interaction potentials, for which
V
is relatively form bounded with respect to the Laplacian. Under these assumptions it can be shown that the BCS functional satisfies the lower bound

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\Tr\bigl[\gamma+(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}})^{2}\gamma\bigr]-C

(1.11)

for some constant
C>0
. In other words, the BCS functional is bounded from below and coercive on the set of admissible BCS states.

The normal state
\Gamma_{0}
is the unique minimizer of the BCS functional when restricted to admissible states with
\alpha=0
and reads

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{0}&0\\
0&1-\overline{\gamma}_{0}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{e}^{((-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{h})^{2}+W_{h}-\mu)/T}}.

(1.12)

Its name is motivated by the fact that it is also the unique minimizer of the BCS functional if the temperature
T
is chosen sufficiently large.
We define the BCS free energy by

\displaystyle F^{\mathrm{BCS}}(h,T)\coloneqq\inf\bigl\{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma_{0}):\Gamma\text{ admissible}\bigr\}

(1.13)

and say that the system is superconducting at temperature
T
if
F^{\mathrm{BCS}}(h,T)<0
. Although it is not difficult to prove that the BCS functional has a minimizer, we refrain from giving a proof here. If we assume that the BCS functional has a minimizer
\Gamma
then the condition
F^{\mathrm{BCS}}(h,T)<0
implies
\alpha=\Gamma_{12}\neq 0
.

The goal of this paper is to derive an asymptotic formula for
F^{\mathrm{BCS}}(h,T)
for small
h>0
. This will allow us to derive Ginzburg–Landau theory and to show how the critical temperature depends on the external electric and magnetic field and on
h
. For our main results to hold, we need the following assumptions.

Assumption 1.1
.

We assume that the interaction potential
V
is a radial function that satisfies
(1+|\cdot|^{2})V\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Moreover, the electric and the magnetic potentials
W\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
A\in W^{3,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
are
\Lambda_{1}
-periodic functions, i.e.
W(x+\lambda)=W(x)
and
A(x+\lambda)=A(x)
for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We also assume that
A(0)=0
.

1.4
The translation-invariant BCS functional

In the absence of external fields we describe the system by translation-invariant states, that is, we assume that the integral kernels of
\gamma
and
\alpha
are of the form
\gamma(x-y)
and
\alpha(x-y)
. The trace per unit volume is in this case defined with respect to a cube with sidelength
1
. We denote the resulting translation-invariant BCS functional by
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathrm{ti},T}
. The translation-invariant BCS functional is studied in detail in
[
32
]
, see also the review article
[
31
]
. In
[
32
]
it has been shown that there is a unique critical temperature
{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\geqslant 0
such that
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{\mathrm{ti},T}
has a minimizer with
\alpha\neq 0
for
T<{T_{\mathrm{c}}}
. The normal state in (
1.12
) with
h=0
is the unique minimizer if
T\geqslant{T_{\mathrm{c}}}
. Moreover, the critical temperature
{T_{\mathrm{c}}}
can be characterized by a linear criterion: It equals the unique temperature
T
such that the linear operator

\displaystyle K_{T}-V

has zero as its lowest eigenvalue. Here
K_{T}=K_{T}(-\mathrm{i}\nabla)
with the symbol

\displaystyle K_{T}(p)\coloneqq\frac{p^{2}-\mu}{\tanh\frac{p^{2}-\mu}{2T}}.

(1.14)

The operator
K_{T}-V
is understood to act on the space
L_{\mathrm{sym}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
of reflection-symmetric square-integrable functions on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. To be precise, the results in
[
32
]
have been proven without the assumption
\alpha(-x)=\alpha(x)
for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In this case, the operator
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V
acts on functions in the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
instead of
L_{\mathrm{sym}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. The results in
[
32
]
, however, equally hold in the case of symmetric Cooper pair wave functions. That is, they hold in the same way if
V
is reflection symmetric and if the translation-invariant BCS functional is minimized over functions
\gamma(x)
and
\alpha(x)
that are both assumed to be reflection symmetric.

We note that the function
K_{T}(p)
satisfies the inequalities
K_{T}(p)\geqslant 2T
for
\mu\geqslant 0
, as well as
K_{T}(p)\geqslant|\mu|/\tanh(|\mu|/(2T))
for
\mu<0
. Our assumptions on
V
guarantee that the essential spectrum of
K_{T}-V
equals that of
K_{T}
, and hence an eigenvalue below
2T
for
\mu\geqslant 0
or below
|\mu|/\tanh(|\mu|/(2T))
for
\mu<0
is necessarily isolated and of finite multiplicity. This, in particular, applies to an eigenvalue of
K_{T}-V
at
0
.

We are interested in the situation, where
{T_{\mathrm{c}}}>0
and where the translation-invariant BCS functional has a unique minimizer with a radial Cooper pair wave function (s-wave Cooper pairs) for
T
close to
{T_{\mathrm{c}}}
. The following assumptions guarantee that we are in such a situation. Part (b) should be compared to
[
15
, Theorem 2.8]
.

Assumption 1.2
.

We assume that the interaction potential
V
is such that the following holds:

(a)

We have
{T_{\mathrm{c}}}>0
.

(b)

The lowest eigenvalue of
K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}-V
is simple.

As has been shown in
[
32
, Theorem 3]
, our first assumption is satisfied if
V\geqslant 0
does not vanish identically. Throughout this paper we denote by
\alpha_{*}
the unique solution to the equation

\displaystyle K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\alpha_{*}=V\alpha_{*}.

(1.15)

Since
V
is radial we know that the same is true for
\alpha_{*}
. Without loss of generality we will assume that
\alpha_{*}
is real-valued and satisfies
\|\alpha_{*}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}=1
. If we write the above equation as
\alpha_{*}=K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}^{-1}V\alpha_{*}
, we see that
V\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
implies
\alpha_{*}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Moreover, we know from
[
21
, Proposition 2]
that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}x\;\bigl[|x^{\nu}\alpha_{*}(x)|^{2}+|x^{\nu}\nabla\alpha_{*}(x)|^{2}\bigr]<\infty

(1.16)

holds for
\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}
.

1.5
The Ginzburg–Landau functional

We say that a function
\Psi
on
Q_{h}
is
gauge-periodic
if the magnetic translations of the form

\displaystyle T_{h}(\lambda)\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(\lambda\wedge X)}\;\Psi(X+\lambda),

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\in\Lambda_{h},

(1.17)

leave
\Psi
invariant. We highlight that
T(\lambda)
in (
1.3
) equals
T_{h}(\lambda)
provided we replace
\mathbf{B}
by
2\mathbf{B}
. Let
\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{2},\Lambda_{3}>0
,
\Lambda_{1},D\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
\Psi
be a gauge-periodic function in the case
h=1
. The Ginzburg–Landau functional is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D}(\Psi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{|Q_{1}|}\int_{Q_{1}}\mathrm{d}X\;\bigl\{\Lambda_{0}\;|(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+2\mathbf{A})\Psi(X)|^{2}+\Lambda_{1}\,W(X)\,|\Psi(X)|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt-D\,\Lambda_{2}\,|\Psi(X)|^{2}+\Lambda_{3}\,|\Psi(X)|^{4}\bigr\}.

(1.18)

We highlight the factor
2
in front of the magnetic vector potential in (
1.18
). Its appearance is due to the fact that
\Psi
describes the center-of-mass motion of Cooper pairs carrying twice the charge of a single fermion.

The Ginzburg–Landau energy is defined by

\displaystyle E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)\coloneqq\inf\bigl\{\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D}(\Psi):\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{1})\bigr\}.

We also define the critical parameter

\displaystyle{D_{\mathrm{c}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\Lambda_{2}}\inf\spec_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{1})}\bigl(\Lambda_{0}\,(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A})^{2}+\Lambda_{1}\;W\bigr).

(1.19)

As has been shown in
[
22
, Lemma 2.5]
, we have
E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)<0
if
D>{D_{\mathrm{c}}}
and
E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)=0
if
D\leqslant{D_{\mathrm{c}}}
.

In our analysis we encounter the Ginzburg–Landau functional in an
h
-dependent version, where
Q_{1},\mathbf{A},W
, and
D
in (
1.18
) are replaced by
Q_{h},\mathbf{A}_{h},W_{h}
, and
h^{2}D
respectively. If we denote this functional by
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,h}(\Psi)
we have

\inf\bigl\{\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,h}(\Psi):\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})\bigr\}=h^{4}E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D),

which follows by scaling. More precisely, for given
\psi
the function

\displaystyle\Psi(X)

\displaystyle\coloneqq h\;\psi(h\,X),

\displaystyle X\in\mathbb{R}^{3},

(1.20)

obeys

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,h}(\Psi)=h^{4}\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D}(\psi).

(1.21)

1.6
Main results

Our first main result concerns an asymptotic expansion of the BCS free energy in the small parameter
h>0
. The precise statement is captured in the following theorem.

Theorem 1
.

Let Assumptions
1.1
and
1.2
hold, let
D\in\mathbb{R}
, and let the coefficients
\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
, and
\Lambda_{3}
be given by (
3.20
)-(
3.23
) below. Then there are constants
C>0
and
h_{0}>0
such that for all
0<h\leqslant h_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle F^{\mathrm{BCS}}(h,\,{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(1-Dh^{2}))=h^{4}\;\bigl(E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)+R\bigr),

(1.22)

with
R
satisfying the estimate

\displaystyle Ch\geqslant R\geqslant-\mathcal{R}\coloneqq-Ch^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{6}}}.

(1.23)

Moreover, for any approximate minimizer
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}
at
T={T_{\mathrm{c}}}(1-Dh^{2})
in the sense that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma_{0})\leqslant h^{4}\bigl(E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)+\rho\bigr)

(1.24)

holds for some
\rho\geqslant 0
, we have the decomposition

\displaystyle\alpha(X,r)=\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)+\sigma(X,r)

(1.25)

for the Cooper pair wave function
\alpha=\Gamma_{12}
. Here,
\sigma
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|Q_{h}|}\int_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;|\sigma(X,r)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant Ch^{\nicefrac{{11}}{{3}}},

(1.26)

\alpha_{*}
is the normalized zero energy eigenstate of
K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}-V
, and the function
\Psi
obeys

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,h}(\Psi)\leqslant h^{4}\left(E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)+\rho+\mathcal{R}\right).

(1.27)

Our second main result is a statement about the dependence of the critical temperature of the BCS functional on
h>0
and on the external fields.

Theorem 2
.

Let Assumptions
1.1
and
1.2
hold. Then there are constants
C>0
and
h_{0}>0
such that for all
0<h\leqslant h_{0}
the following holds:

(a)

Let
0<T_{0}<{T_{\mathrm{c}}}
. If the temperature
T
satisfies

T_{0}\leqslant T\leqslant{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\,(1-h^{2}\,({D_{\mathrm{c}}}+C\,h^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}))

(1.28)

with
{D_{\mathrm{c}}}
in (
1.19
), then we have

F^{\mathrm{BCS}}(h,T)<0.

(b)

If the temperature
T
satisfies

T\geqslant{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\,(1-h^{2}\,({D_{\mathrm{c}}}-\mathcal{R}))

(1.29)

with
{D_{\mathrm{c}}}
in (
1.19
) and
\mathcal{R}
in (
1.23
), then we have

\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma_{0})>0

unless
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}
.

Remarks 1.3
.

(a)

Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
extend similar results in
[
21
]
and
[
22
]
to the case of general external electric and magnetic fields. In these references the main restriction is that the vector potential is assumed to be periodic, that is, the corresponding magnetic field has vanishing flux through the faces of the unit cell
Q_{h}
, compare with the discussion in Section
1.2
. Removing this restriction causes major mathematical difficulties already for the constant magnetic field because its vector potential cannot be treated as a perturbation of the Laplacian. More precisely, it was possible in
[
21
,
22
]
to work with a priori bounds for low-energy states that do not involve the external magnetic field. As noticed in the discussion below Remark 6 in
[
19
]
, this is not possible if the magnetic field has nonzero flux through the faces of the unit cell. To prove a priori bounds that involve a constant magnetic field one has to deal with the fact that the components of the magnetic momentum operator do not commute, which leads to significant technical difficulties. These difficulties have been overcome in
[
16
]
, which allowed us to extend the results
[
21
,
22
]
to the case of a system in a constant magnetic field. Our proof of Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
uses these a priori bounds, and should therefore be interpreted as an extension of the methods in
[
16
]
to the case of general external electric and magnetic fields. The main technical novelty of this article is a further development of the phase approximation method, which has been pioneered in the framework of BCS theory for the case of the constant magnetic field in
[
19
]
and
[
16
]
. It allows us to compute the BCS free energy of a class of trial states (Gibbs states) in a controlled way, and is the key new ingredient for our proof of upper and lower bounds for the BCS free energy in the presence of general external fields.

(b)

When we compare our result in Theorem
1
to the main Theorem in
[
21
]
, we notice the following differences: (1) We use microscopic coordinates while macroscopic coordinates are used in
[
21
,
22
]
, see the discussion above
[
21
, Eq. (1.4)]
. (2) Our free energy is normalized by a large volume factor, see (
1.9
) and (
1.10
). This is not the case in
[
21
,
22
]
. Accordingly, the GL energy appears on the order
h^{4}
in our setting and on the order
h
in the setting in
[
21
]
. (3) The leading order of the Cooper pair wave function in
[
21
, Theorem 1]
is of the form

\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{*}(x-y)(\Psi(x)+\Psi(y)).

(1.30)

This should be compared to (
1.25
), where relative and center-of-mass coordinates are used. When we use the a priori bound for
\|\nabla\Psi\|_{2}
below Eq. (5.61) in
[
21
]
, we see that this decomposition equals that in (
1.25
) to leading order in
h
.

(c)

The Ginzburg–Landau energy appears at the order
h^{4}
. This needs to be compared to the energy of the normal state, which is of order
1
in
h
. To understand the order of the GL energy we need to realize that each factor of
\Psi
in
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,h}
defined below (
1.19
) carries a factor
h
. This follows from the scaling in (
1.20
) and the fact that the GL energy is normalized by the volume factor
|Q_{h}|^{-1}
. Moreover, every magnetic momentum operator carries a factor
h
because
\Psi
varies on the length scale
h^{-1}
and the electric potential carries a factor
h^{2}
. In combination, these considerations explain the size of all terms in the GL functional. It is worth noting that the prefactor
-h^{2}D
in front of the quadratic term without external fields equals
(T-T_{\mathrm{c}})/T_{\mathrm{c}}
.

(d)

The size of the remainder in (
1.26
) should be compared to the
L^{2}
-norm per unit volume of the leading order part of the Cooper pair wave function in (
1.25
), which satisfies

\frac{1}{|Q_{h}|}\int_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;|\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)|^{2}\sim h^{2}

if
D>0
. We highlight that
\alpha_{*}
varies on the microscopic length scale
1
and that
\Psi
captures the effects of the external fields on the macroscopic length scale
h^{-1}
.

(e)

Our bounds show that
D
in Theorem
1
can be chosen as a function of
h
as long as
|D|\leqslant D_{0}
holds for some constant
D_{0}>0
.

(f)

The upper bound for the error in (
1.23
) is worse than the corresponding bound in
[
16
]
by the factor
h^{-1}
. It is of the same size as the comparable error term in
[
21
, Theorem 1]
.

(g)

Theorem
2
gives bounds on the temperature regions where superconductivity is present or absent. The interpretation of the theorem is that the critical temperature of the full model satisfies

{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(h)={T_{\mathrm{c}}}\left(1-D_{\mathrm{c}}h^{2}\right)+o(h^{2}),

with the critical temperature
{T_{\mathrm{c}}}
of the translation-invariant problem. The coefficient
D_{\mathrm{c}}
is determined by linearized Ginzburg–Landau theory, see (
1.17
). The above equation allows us to compute the upper critical field
B_{\mathrm{c}2}
, above which superconductivity is absent. It also allows to to compute the derivative of
B_{\mathrm{c}2}
with respect to
T
at
{T_{\mathrm{c}}}
, see
[
19
, Appendix A]
.

(h)

We expect that the assumption
0<T_{0}<{T_{\mathrm{c}}}
in part (a) of Theorem
2
, which also appeared in
[
19
,
16
]
, is only of technical nature. We need it because our trial state analysis breaks down as
T
approaches zero. We note that there is no such restriction in part (b) of Theorem
2
or in Theorem
1
.

1.7
Organization of the paper and strategy of proof

For the convenience of the reader we give here a short summary of the organization of the paper and the proof of our two main results.

In Section
1.8
we provide a brief non-rigorous computation that shows from which terms in the BCS functional the different terms in the GL functional arise. Afterwards we complete in Section
2
the introduction of our mathematical setup. That is, we collect useful properties of the trace per unit volume and introduce the relevant spaces of gauge-periodic functions.

In Section
3
we collect the results of our trial state analysis. We introduce a class of Gibbs states with Cooper pair wave functions that admit a product structure of the form
\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)
to leading order in
h
. Here,
\alpha_{*}
is the ground state wave function in (
1.15
) and
\Psi
is a gauge-periodic function. We state and motivate several results concerning the Cooper pair wave function and the BCS energy of these states. Afterwards we use these statements to provide the proof of the upper bound for the BCS free energy in (
1.22
) as well as the proof of Theorem
2
(a). It is important to note that these results are needed again in Section
6
, where we give the proof of the lower bound on the BCS free energy in (
1.22
) and the proof of Theorem
2
(b).

In Section
4
we provide the proofs of the results in Section
3
concerning the Cooper pair wave function and the BCS free energy of our trial states. It is the main part of our article and contains the main technical novelties. Our approach is based on an application of the phase approximation method for general magnetic fields to our nonlinear setting. The phase approximation is a well-known tool in the physics literature, see, e.g.,
[
37
]
, and has also been used in the mathematical literature to study spectral properties of Schrödinger operators involving a magnetic field, for instance in
[
9
,
42
]
. In the case of a constant magnetic field, this method has been pioneered within the framework of linearized BCS theory in
[
19
]
and for the full nonlinear model in
[
16
]
. An application of the phase approximation method to the case of a magnetic field with zero flux through the unit cell is contained in unpublished notes by Frank, Geisinger, Hainzl, and Tzaneteas
[
17
]
. The main technical novelty in Section
4
is a further development of the phase approximation method for general external fields in our nonlinear setting. This allows us to compute the BCS free energy of a class of trial states (Gibbs states) in a controlled way, which is the key new ingredient for the proof of upper and lower bounds for the BCS free energy in the presence of general external fields. Our approach should also be compared to the trial state analysis in
[
21
,
22
]
, where a semi-classical expansion is used to treat magnetic fields with zero flux through the unit cell. We highlight that the trial state analysis for general external fields requires considerably more effort than the one for a constant magnetic field in
[
16
]
. This is also reflected in the length of the proofs.

In Section
5
, we prove a priori estimates for BCS states, whose BCS free energy is smaller than or equal to that of the normal state
\Gamma_{0}
in (
1.12
) (low-energy states). More precisely, we show that the Cooper pair wave function of any such state is, to leading order as
h\to 0
, given by
\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)
with
\alpha_{*}
in (
1.15
) and with a gauge-periodic function
\Psi
. The proof of the same statement in the case of a constant magnetic field has been the main novelty in
[
16
]
. To treat the case of general external fields, we perturbatively remove the periodic vector potential
A
and the electric potential
W
. This allows us to reduce the problem to the case of a constant magnetic field treated in
[
16
]
. Similar a priori estimates for the case of magnetic fields with zero flux through the unit cell had been proved for the first time in
[
21
]
.

The proofs of the lower bound on (
1.22
) and of Theorem
2
(b), which go along the same lines as those presented in
[
21
,
22
,
16
]
, are given in Section
6
. They complete the proofs of Theorem
1
and
2
. The main idea is to use the a priori estimates in Section
5
to replace a general low-energy state in the BCS functional by a Gibbs state, whose Cooper pair wave function has the same leading order behavior for small
h
, in a controlled way. This, in particluar, allows us to estimate the BCS energy of a general low-energy state in terms of that of a Gibbs state, which has been computed in Sections
3
and
4
. Because of the considerable overlap in content with the related section in
[
16
]
, we shortened the proofs in this section to a minimal length.

Throughout the paper,
c
and
C
denote generic positive constants that change from line to line. We allow them to depend on the various fixed quantities like
h_{0}
,
D_{0}
,
\mu
,
{T_{\mathrm{c}}}
,
V
,
A
,
W
,
\alpha_{*}
, etc. Further dependencies are highlighted.

1.8
Heuristic computation of the terms in the Ginzburg–Landau functional

In the following we present a brief and non-rigorous computation of the BCS energy of the trial state that we use in the proof of the upper bound for the BCS free energy in Section
3
. The goal is to show from which terms in the BCS functional the different terms in the Ginzburg–Landau (GL) functional arise. A more detailed and more precise discussion of these issues can be found in Section
3
.

Our trial state (a Gibbs state) is defined by

\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{\Delta}&\alpha_{\Delta}\\
\overline{\alpha_{\Delta}}&1-\overline{\gamma_{\Delta}}\end{pmatrix}=\Gamma_{\Delta}=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta H_{\Delta}}}.

(1.31)

Here,
\beta^{-1}=T={T_{\mathrm{c}}}(1-Dh^{2})
and the Hamiltonian is given by

H_{\Delta}=\begin{pmatrix}(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{h})^{2}-\mu&\Delta\\
\overline{\Delta}&-\overline{(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{h})^{2}}+\mu\end{pmatrix},

where the operator
\Delta
is defined via its integral kernel

\Delta(x,y)=-2\,V\alpha_{*}(x-y)\,\Psi_{h}\left(\frac{x+y}{2}\right).

The function
\Psi_{h}(X)=h\psi(hX)
is chosen such that
\psi
is a minimizer of the Ginzburg–Landau functional in (
1.18
). We therefore have

\frac{1}{|Q_{h}|}\int_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\ |\Psi_{h}(X)|^{2}\sim h^{2}

as well as

\Tr[\Delta^{*}\Delta]=\frac{4}{|Q_{h}|}\int_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi_{h}(X)|^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;|V(r)\alpha_{*}(r)|^{2}\sim h^{2}.

(1.32)

Here
r=x-y
and
X=(x+y)/2
denote relative and center-of-mass coordinates. The operator
\Delta
in (
1.31
) is therefore a small perturbation if
0<h\ll 1
.

The BCS free energy of the trial state
\Gamma_{\Delta}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma_{\Delta})-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Tr\left[H_{0}(\Gamma_{\Delta}-\Gamma_{0})\right]-TS(\Gamma)+TS(\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt-\frac{1}{|Q_{h}|}\int_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;V(r)|\alpha_{\Delta}(X,r)|^{2},

where
\Gamma_{0}
denotes the normal state in (
1.12
). Applications of

\Tr\left[H_{0}(\Gamma_{\Delta}-\Gamma_{0})\right]=\Tr\left[H_{\Delta}\Gamma_{\Delta}-H_{0}\Gamma_{0}\right]-\Tr[(H_{\Delta}-H_{0})\Gamma_{\Delta}],

and
[
21
, Eqs. (4.3-4.5)]
, allow us to rewrite this formula as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma_{\Delta})-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma_{0})=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\beta}\Tr_{0}\bigl[\ln\bigl(1+\exp\bigr(-\beta H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr)-\ln\bigl(1+\exp\bigl(-\beta H_{0}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle+\frac{\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}{|Q_{h}|}\int_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\ |\Psi_{h}(X)|^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{|Q_{h}|}\int_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;V(r)\,\bigl|\alpha(X,r)-\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi_{h}(X)\bigr|^{2}.

(1.33)

Here
\Tr_{0}[A]=\Tr[PAP]+\Tr[QAQ]
with

P=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

and
Q=1-P
.

To identify the terms in the GL functional, we need to expand the terms in (
1.33
) in powers of
h
. To that end, we first expand them up to fourth order in powers of
\Delta
because the Ginzburg–Landau functional is a fourth order polynomial in
\Psi_{h}
. Afterwards, we expand the resulting terms in powers of
h
, that is, we use that the external fields
W_{h}(x)=h^{2}W(hx)
and
\mathbf{A}_{h}(x)=h\mathbf{A}(hx)
as well as the temperature
T={T_{\mathrm{c}}}(1-Dh^{2})
with
D>0
depend on
h
. It turns out that the last term on the right side of (
1.33
) is of the order
o(h^{4})
. Since the GL energy appears on the order
h^{4}
it does not contribute to it. In our trial state analysis in Sections
3
and
4
we show that there is a linear operator
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
and a cubic map
N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)
such that

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\beta}\Tr_{0}\bigl[\ln\bigl(1+\exp\bigl(-\beta H_{\Delta}\bigr)\bigr)-\ln\bigl(1+\exp\bigl(-\beta H_{0}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip-120.0pt=-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}\Delta\rangle+\frac{1}{8}\langle\Delta,N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)\rangle+o(h^{4})

(1.34)

holds. In combination with the first term in the second line of (
1.33
), the quadratic terms in (
1.34
) contain the quadratic terms in the Ginzburg–Landau functional:

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}\Delta\rangle+\frac{\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}{|Q_{h}|}\int_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi_{h}(X)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|Q_{h}|}\int_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\;\bigl\{\Lambda_{0}\;|(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+2\mathbf{A}_{h})\Psi_{h}(X)|^{2}+\Lambda_{1}\,W_{h}(X)\,|\Psi_{h}(X)|^{2}-Dh^{2}\,\Lambda_{2}\,|\Psi_{h}(X)|^{2}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+o(h^{4})

with
\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1},
and
\Lambda_{2}
defined in (
3.20
)-(
3.22
). From the quartic term in (
1.34
) we will extract the quartic term in the GL functional:

\frac{1}{8}\langle\Delta,N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)\rangle=\frac{\Lambda_{3}}{|Q_{h}|}\int_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi_{h}(X)|^{4}+o(h^{4}).

The coefficient
\Lambda_{3}
is defined in (
3.23
). Accordingly,

\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma_{\Delta})-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma_{0})=h^{4}\left(E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)+o(1)\right),

where we used
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,h}(\Psi_{h})=h^{4}E^{\mathrm{GL}}(D)
in the last step.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Schatten classes

The trace per unit volume in (
1.9
) gives rise to Schatten classes of periodic operators, whose norms play an important role in our proofs. In this section we recall several well-known facts about these norms.

For
1\leqslant p<\infty
, the
p
{}^{\textrm{th}}
local von-Neumann–Schatten class
\mathcal{S}^{p}
consists of all gauge-periodic operators
A
having finite
p
-norm, that is,
\|A\|_{p}^{p}\coloneqq\Tr(|A|^{p})<\infty
. The space of bounded gauge-periodic operators
\mathcal{S}^{\infty}
is equipped with the usual operator norm. We note that the
p
-norm is not monotone decreasing in the index
p
. This should be compared to the usual Schatten norms, where such a property holds, see the discussion below
[
21
, Eq. (3.9)]
.

We recall that the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\|A+B\|_{p}\leqslant\|A\|_{p}+\|B\|_{p}

holds for operators in
\mathcal{S}^{p}
for
1\leqslant p\leqslant\infty
. We also have the generalized version of Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle\|AB\|_{r}\leqslant\|A\|_{p}\|B\|_{q},

(2.1)

which holds for
1\leqslant p,q,r\leqslant\infty
with
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}
. The familiar inequality

\displaystyle|\Tr A|\leqslant\|A\|_{1}

also holds in the case of local Schatten norms.

The above inequalities can be deduced from their versions for the usual Schatten norms, see, e.g.,
[
48
]
, with the help of the magnetic Bloch–Floquet decomposition. We refer to
[
44
, Section XIII.16]
for an introduction to the Bloch–Floquet transformation and to
[
27
]
for a treatment of the magnetic case. To be more precise, a gauge-periodic operator
A
satisfies the unitary equivalence

\displaystyle A\cong\int^{\oplus}_{[0,\sqrt{2\pi}\,h]^{3}}\mathrm{d}k\;A_{k},

which we use to write the trace per unit volume as

\displaystyle\Tr A=\int_{[0,\sqrt{2\pi}\,h]^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\Tr_{L^{2}(Q_{h})}A_{k}.

(2.2)

Here,
\Tr_{L^{2}(Q_{h})}
denotes the usual trace over
L^{2}(Q_{h})
. When we use that
(AB)_{k}=A_{k}B_{k}
holds for gauge-periodic operators
A
and
B
, the above mentioned inequalities for the trace per unit volume are implied by their usual versions.

2.2
Gauge-periodic Sobolev spaces

In this section we introduce Banach spaces of gauge-periodic functions, which will be used to describe Cooper pair wave functions of BCS states.

When working with center-of-mass and relative coordinates
(X,r)
it is useful to define the magnetic momentum operators

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathbf{A}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X}+2\mathbf{A}(X),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{\mathbf{A}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{A}(r).

(2.3)

We will also use the notation

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X}+2\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}(X),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}(r).

(2.4)

If several coordinates appear in an equation we sometimes write
\Pi_{X}
and
\widetilde{\pi}_{r}
to highlight on which coordinate
\Pi
and
\widetilde{\pi}
are acting.

A function
\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
1\leqslant p\leqslant\infty
belongs to the space
L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{p}(Q_{h})
provided
T_{h}(\lambda)\Psi=\Psi
holds for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{h}
(with
T_{h}(\lambda)
in (
1.17
)). We endow
L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{p}(Q_{h})
with the usual
p
-norm per unit volume

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{p}(Q_{h})}^{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\fint_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi(X)|^{p}\coloneqq\frac{1}{|Q_{h}|}\int_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Psi(X)|^{p}

(2.5)

if
1\leqslant p<\infty
and with the
L^{\infty}(Q_{h})
-norm if
p=\infty
. When it does not lead to confusion we use the abbreviation
\|\Psi\|_{p}
.

Analogously, for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we define the Sobolev spaces of gauge-periodic functions corresponding to the constant magnetic field as

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{m}(Q_{h})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigl\{\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h}):\Pi^{\nu}\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})\quad\forall\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3},|\nu|_{1}\leqslant m\bigr\},

(2.6)

where
|\nu|_{1}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{3}\nu_{i}
for
\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}
. It is a Hilbert space when endowed with the inner product

\displaystyle\langle\Phi,\Psi\rangle_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{m}(Q_{h})}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{|\nu|_{1}\leqslant m}h^{-2-2|\nu|_{1}}\;\langle\Pi^{\nu}\Phi,\Pi^{\nu}\Psi\rangle_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})}.

(2.7)

We note that if
\Psi
is a gauge-periodic function then so is
\Pi^{\nu}\Psi
, since the magnetic momentum operator
\Pi

commutes with the magnetic translations
T_{h}(\lambda)
in (
1.17
). Furthermore,
\Pi
is a self-adjoint operator on
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})
.

The norms introduced in (
2.5
) and (
2.7
) display a scaling behavior with respect to
h
, which is motivated by the Ginzburg–Landau scaling in (
1.20
). More precisely, whenever
\psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{p}(Q_{1})
and
\Psi(x)=h\psi(hx)
, then

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{p}(Q_{h})}=h\,\|\psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{p}(Q_{1})}

(2.8)

for every
1\leqslant p\leqslant\infty
. That is,
\Psi\sim h
in any
p
-norm per unit volume.

The inner product in (
2.7
) is chosen such that

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{m}(Q_{h})}=\|\psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{m}(Q_{1})}

holds. This follows from (
2.8
) and the fact that
\|\Pi^{\nu}\Psi\|_{2}^{2}
scales as
h^{2+2|\nu|_{1}}
for
\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}
. Such scaled norms have also been used in
[
16
]
but not in
[
21
,
22
]
.

For the sake of completeness, let us also mention the following magnetic Sobolev inequality. There is a constant
C>0
such that for any
h>0
and any
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})
, we have

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{6}(Q_{h})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,h^{-2}\,\|\Pi\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})}^{2}.

(2.9)

The proof can be found in
[
16
]
below Eq. (2.7).

The Cooper pair wave function
\alpha
of an admissible BCS state
\Gamma
belongs to the Hilbert–Schmidt class
\mathcal{S}^{2}
defined in Section
2.1
, see the discussion below (
1.9
). The symmetry and the gauge-periodicity of the kernel of
\alpha
in (
1.7
) can be reformlated as

\displaystyle\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(\lambda\wedge X)}\;\alpha(X+\lambda,r),\quad\lambda\in\Lambda_{h};

\displaystyle\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle=\alpha(X,-r)

(2.10)

in terms of center-of-mass and relative coordinates. In other words,
\alpha(X,r)
is a gauge-periodic function of the center-of-mass coordinate
X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and a reflection-symmetric function of the relative coordinate
r\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We make use of the unitary equivalence of
\mathcal{S}^{2}
and the space

\displaystyle{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}\coloneqq L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})\otimes L_{\mathrm{sym}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),

which consists of all square-integrable functions satisfying (
2.10
). We also define the norm

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}^{2}\coloneqq\fint_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;|\alpha(X,r)|^{2}=\frac{1}{|Q_{h}|}\int_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;|\alpha(X,r)|^{2}.

The identity
\|\alpha\|_{2}=\|\alpha\|_{{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}
follows from (
2.10
). In the following we therefore identify the scalar products
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
and
\mathcal{S}^{2}
with each other and we do not distinguish between operators in
\mathcal{S}^{2}
and their kernels as this does not lead to confusion.

By
H^{1}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})
we denote the Sobolev space of all functions
\alpha\in L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})
, which have finite
H^{1}
-norm defined by

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{H^{1}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}^{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\|\alpha\|_{2}^{2}+\|\Pi\alpha\|_{2}^{2}+\|\widetilde{\pi}\alpha\|_{2}^{2}

(2.11)

with
\Pi
and
\widetilde{\pi}
in (
2.4
).

We highlight that the norm in (
2.11
) is equivalent to the two norms

\displaystyle\Tr[\alpha\alpha^{*}]+\Tr[(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}})\alpha\alpha^{*}(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}})]+\Tr[(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}})\alpha^{*}\alpha(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}})]

(2.12)

and

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{2}^{2}+\|(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}})\alpha\|_{2}^{2}+\|\alpha(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}})\|_{2}^{2},

(2.13)

compare also with the discussion below (
1.9
). We also note that the
H^{m}
-norm in (
2.7
) and the
H^{1}
-norm in (
2.11
) are equivalent to the norms that we obtain when
\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{B}}
is replaced by
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{B}}+A
with a periodic vector potential
A\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

3
Trial States and their BCS Energy

In this section we introduce a class of trial states (Gibbs states), state several results concerning their Cooper pair wave function and their BCS free energy, and use these results to prove the upper bound on (
1.22
) as well as Theorem
2
(a). The trial states
\Gamma_{\Delta}
are of the form stated in (
1.31
). In Proposition
3.2
we show that if
\Delta
is given by
V\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)
with a gauge periodic function
\Psi
that is small in an appropriate sense for small
h
, then
[\Gamma_{\Delta}]_{12}=\alpha_{\Delta}\approx\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)
to leading order in
h
. In Proposition
3.5
we prove a representation formula for the BCS functional that allows us to compute the BCS energy of the trial states
\Gamma_{\Delta}
. Finally, in Theorem
3.6
we extract the terms of the Ginzburg–Landau functional from the BCS free energy of
\Gamma_{\Delta}
. The proofs of these statements are given in Section
4
. Our trial analysis should be viewed as further development of that in
[
16
]
for the constant magnetic field. The techniques we develop in Sections
3
and
4
are based on gauge-invariant perturbation, which has been pioneered in the framework of linearized BCS theory for a constant external magnetic field in
[
19
]
. Our approach should also be compared to the trial state analysis in
[
21
,
22
]
, where a semi-classical expansion is used to treat magnetic fields with zero flux through the unit cell.

3.1
The Gibbs states
\Gamma_{\Delta}

For
\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})
we define the gap function
\Delta\in{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
by

\displaystyle\Delta(X,r)\coloneqq\Delta_{\Psi}(X,r)

\displaystyle\coloneqq-2\;V\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X).

(3.1)

We also introduce the one-particle Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}+W\coloneqq(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{h})^{2}+W_{h}-\mu

(3.2)

as well as

\displaystyle H_{\Delta}

\displaystyle\coloneqq H_{0}+\delta\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}&0\\
0&-\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&\Delta\\
\overline{\Delta}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}&\Delta\\
\overline{\Delta}&-\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.3)

The Gibbs state at inverse temperature
\beta=T^{-1}>0
is defined by

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{\Delta}&\alpha_{\Delta}\\
\overline{\alpha_{\Delta}}&1-\overline{\gamma_{\Delta}}\end{pmatrix}=\Gamma_{\Delta}\coloneqq\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta H_{\Delta}}}.

(3.4)

We highlight that the choice
\Delta=0
yields the normal state
\Gamma_{0}
in (
1.12
). In our proof of the upper bound for the free energy in (
1.22
) we will choose
\Psi
as a minimizer of the Ginzburg–Landau functional in (
1.18
), which satisfies the scaling in (
1.20
). Since the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-norm of
V\alpha_{*}
is of the order
1
, the local Hilbert–Schmidt norm of
\Delta
is of the order
h
in this case. In the proof of the lower bound we have less information about the function
\Psi
. The related difficulties are discussed in Remark
3.3
below.

Lemma 3.1

(Admissibility of
\Gamma_{\Delta}
)
.

Let Assumptions
1.1
and
1.2
hold. Then, for any
h>0
, any
T>0
, and any
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})
, the state
\Gamma_{\Delta}
in (
3.4
) is admissible.

The choice of the states
\Gamma_{\Delta}
is motivated by the following observation. Using standard variational arguments one can show that any minimizer
\Gamma
of the BCS functional satisfies the nonlinear Bogolubov–de Gennes equation

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\,\mathbb{H}_{V\alpha}}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{V\alpha}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}&-2\,V\alpha\\
-2\,\overline{V\alpha}&-\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.5)

Here,
V\alpha
is the operator given by the integral kernel
V(r)\alpha(X,r)
. Since we are interested in approximate minimizers of the BCS functional, we choose
\Gamma_{\Delta}
as an approximate solution to the BdG-equation in (
3.5
). The next result shows that, as far as the leading order behavior of
\alpha_{\Delta}
is concerned, this is indeed the case. It should be compared to (
1.25
).

Proposition 3.2

(Structure of
\alpha_{\Delta}
)
.

Let Assumption
1.1
and
1.2
(a) be satisfied and let
T_{0}>0
be given. Then, there is a constant
h_{0}>0
such that for any
0<h\leqslant h_{0}
, any
T\geqslant T_{0}
, and any
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})
the function
\alpha_{\Delta}
in (
3.4
) with
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
) has the decomposition

\displaystyle\alpha_{\Delta}(X,r)

\displaystyle=\Psi(X)\alpha_{*}(r)-\eta_{0}(\Delta)(X,r)-\eta_{\perp}(\Delta)(X,r).

(3.6)

The remainder functions
\eta_{0}(\Delta)
and
\eta_{\perp}(\Delta)
have the following properties:

(a)

The function
\eta_{0}
satisfies the bound

\displaystyle\|\eta_{0}\|_{H^{1}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;\bigl(h^{5}+h^{2}\,|T-{T_{\mathrm{c}}}|^{2}\bigr)\;\bigl(\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{6}+\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}\bigr).

(3.7)

(b)

The function
\eta_{\perp}
satisfies the bound

\displaystyle\|\eta_{\perp}\|_{{H^{1}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}^{2}+\||r|\eta_{\perp}\|_{{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;h^{6}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})}^{2}.

(3.8)

(c)

The function
\eta_{\perp}
has the explicit form

\displaystyle\eta_{\perp}(X,r)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\,V\alpha_{*}(s)\,\bigl[\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)-1\bigr]\Psi(X)

with
k_{T}(Z,r)
defined in Section
4
below (
4.108
). Moreover, for any radial
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
the operator

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\;f(r)\,k_{T}(Z,r-s)\,g(s)\,\bigl[\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)-1\bigr]

commutes with
\Pi^{2}
. In particular,
if
P
and
Q
are two spectral projections of
\Pi^{2}
with
PQ=0
, then
\eta_{\perp}
satisfies the orthogonality property

\displaystyle\bigl\langle f(r)\,(P\Psi)(X),\,\eta_{\perp}(\Delta_{Q\Psi})\bigr\rangle=0.

(3.9)

Remark 3.3
.

The statement of Proposition
3.2
should be read in two different ways, depending on whether we are interested in proving the upper or the lower bound for the BCS free energy. In the former case, the bound on
\||r|\eta_{\perp}\|_{{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}}
in part (b) and part (c) are irrelevant. The reason is that the gap function
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
is defined with a minimizer
\Psi
of the GL functional, whose
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{B})
-norm is uniformly bounded. In this case all remainder terms can be estimated using (
3.7
) and (
3.8
).

In contrast, in the proof of the lower bound for the BCS free energy in Section
6
we are forced to work with a trial state
\Gamma_{\Delta}
, whose gap function is defined in terms of a function
\Psi
that is related to a low-energy state of the BCS functional. The properties of such a function are captured in Theorem
5.1
below. In this case we only have a bound on the
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})
-norm of
\Psi
at our disposal. To obtain a function in
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})
, we introduce a regularized version of
\Psi
as in
[
21
, Section 6]
,
[
22
, Section 6]
,
[
19
, Section 7]
, and
[
16
, Section 6]
by
\Psi_{\leqslant}\coloneqq\mathbbs 1_{[0,\varepsilon]}(\Pi^{2})\Psi
for some
h^{2}\ll\varepsilon\ll 1
, see Corollary
5.2
. The
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})
-norm of
\Psi_{\leqslant}
is not uniformly bounded in
h
, see (
5.5
) below. This causes a certain error term in the proof of the lower bound to be large, a priori. Part (b) and (c) of Proposition
3.2
are needed to overcome this problem. Since many details of the relevant proof in Section
6
have been omitted because they go along the same lines as those in
[
16
, Section 6]
we refer to
[
16
, Remark 3.3]
for more details.

3.2
The BCS energy of the states
\Gamma_{\Delta}

In this section we compute the BCS free energy of our trial states
\Gamma_{\Delta}
. The goal is to show that this energy minus the energy of the normal state
\Gamma_{0}
is, to leading order as
h\to 0
, given by the Ginzburg–Landau energy of the function
\Psi
appearing in the definition of
\Delta
. For a brief heuristic summary of these computations we refer to Section
1.8
.

We start our discussion by introducing the operators
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
and
N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
that naturally appear when the BCS energy of
\Gamma_{\Delta}
is expanded in powers of the gap function
\Delta
, see (
1.34
). The Matsubara frequencies are given by

\displaystyle\omega_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\pi(2n+1)T,\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

(3.10)

For a local Hilbert–Schmidt operator
\Delta
with integral kernel
\Delta(x,y)
satisfying (
1.7
) and
\Delta(x,y)=\Delta(y,x)
we define the linear map
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
by

\displaystyle L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}\Delta

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}}.

(3.11)

The operator
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}
is defined in (
3.2
).
In the parameter regime we are interested in, we obtain the quadratic terms in the Ginzburg–Landau functional from
\langle\Delta,L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}\Delta\rangle
. The spectral properties of the operator
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
have been studied in great detail for
W=0
and
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{e_{3}}
in
[
19
]
. This allows the authors to compute the BCS critical temperature shift caused by a small constant magnetic field within the framework of linearized BCS theory. That this prediction is accurate also if the nonlinear problem is considered has been shown in
[
16
]
.

Moreover, the nonlinear (cubic) map
N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
is defined by

\displaystyle N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}}\,\overline{\Delta}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}}.

(3.12)

The expression
\langle\Delta,N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)\rangle
gives rise to the quartic term in the Ginzburg–Landau functional. The operator
N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
also appeared in
[
17
]
and in
[
16
]
.

From the following lemma we know that
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}\Delta
and
N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)(\Delta)
are both in
{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
provided
\Delta
satisfies the symmetry relations in (
2.10
) and some mild regularity assumptions.

Lemma 3.4
.

The map
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
is a bounded linear operator on
{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
. Assume that the integral kernel
\Delta\in L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})
defines a bounded operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Then we have
N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)\in{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
.

The following representation formula for the BCS functional is the starting point of our proofs of Theorems
1
and
2
. It will be used to prove upper and lower bounds, and is therefore formulated for general BCS states and not only for our trial states. The proof of Proposition
3.5
can be found in
[
16
, Proposition 3.4]
.

Proposition 3.5

(Representation formula for the BCS functional)
.

Let
\Gamma
be an admissible state. For any
h>0
, let
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})
and let
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
be as in (
3.1
). For
T>0
and if
V\alpha_{*}\in L^{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, there is an operator
\mathcal{R}_{T,\mathbf{A},W}^{(1)}(\Delta)\in\mathcal{S}^{1}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle\hskip-70.0pt=-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}\Delta\rangle+\frac{1}{8}\langle\Delta,N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)\rangle+\|\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})}\,\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\hskip-40.0pt+\Tr\bigl[\mathcal{R}_{T,\mathbf{A},W}^{(1)}(\Delta)\bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip-40.0pt+\frac{T}{2}\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Gamma,\Gamma_{\Delta})-\fint_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;V(r)\,\bigl|\alpha(X,r)-\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)\bigr|^{2},

(3.13)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Gamma,\Gamma_{\Delta})\coloneqq\Tr_{0}\bigl[\Gamma(\ln\Gamma-\ln\Gamma_{\Delta})+(1-\Gamma)(\ln(1-\Gamma)-\ln(1-\Gamma_{\Delta}))\bigr]

(3.14)

denotes the relative entropy of
\Gamma
with respect to
\Gamma_{\Delta}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{R}_{T,\mathbf{A},W}^{(1)}(\Delta)
obeys the estimate

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{T,\mathbf{A},W}^{(1)}(\Delta)\|_{1}\leqslant C\;T^{-5}\;h^{6}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{6}.

The definition (
3.14
) of the relative entropy uses a weaker form of trace called
\Tr_{0}
, which is defined as follows. We call a gauge-periodic operator
A
acting on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
weakly locally trace class if
P_{0}AP_{0}
and
Q_{0}AQ_{0}
are locally trace class, where

\displaystyle P_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

(3.15)

and
Q_{0}=1-P_{0}
. For such operators the weak trace per unit volume is defined by

\displaystyle\Tr_{0}(A)\coloneqq\Tr\bigl(P_{0}AP_{0}+Q_{0}AQ_{0}\bigr).

(3.16)

If an operator
A
is locally trace class then it is also weakly locally trace class but the converse statement does not hold in general. The converse is true, however, if
A\geqslant 0
. We highlight that if
A
is locally trace class then the weak trace per unit volume and the trace per unit volume coincide. Before their appearance in the context of BCS theory in
[
21
,
22
,
16
]
, weak traces of the above kind appeared in
[
33
,
25
]
.

Let us have a closer look at the right side of (
3.13
). From the terms in the first line we will extract the Ginzburg–Landau functional, see Theorem
3.6
below. The terms in the second and third line contribute to the remainder. The term in the second line is small in absolute value, but the techniques used to bound the third line differ for upper and lower bounds. This is responsible for the different qualities of the upper and lower bounds in Theorems
1
and
2
, see (
1.23
). For an upper bound we choose
\Gamma\coloneqq\Gamma_{\Delta}
. Hence
\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Gamma_{\Delta},\Gamma_{\Delta})=0
and the last term in (
3.13
) can be estimated with the help of Proposition
3.2
. To obtain a lower bound, the third line needs to be bounded from below using the lower bound for the relative entropy in
[
16
, Lemma 6.1]
, which appeared for the first time in
[
21
, Lemma 5]
.

Before we state the next result, we introduce the function

\displaystyle\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}(p)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}x\;\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}p\cdot x}\,V(x)\alpha_{*}(x),

(3.17)

which also fixes our convention of the Fourier transform.

Theorem 3.6

(Calculation of the GL energy)
.

Let Assumptions
1.1
and
1.2
(a) hold and let
D\in\mathbb{R}
be given. Then, there is a constant
h_{0}>0
such that for any
0<h\leqslant h_{0}
, any
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})
,
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
), and
T={T_{\mathrm{c}}}(1-Dh^{2})
, we have

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\langle\Delta,L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}\Delta\rangle+\frac{1}{8}\langle\Delta,N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)\rangle+\|\Psi\|_{L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})}^{2}\;\langle\alpha_{*},V\alpha_{*}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\hskip-60.0pt=\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,h}(\Psi)+R(h).

(3.18)

Here,

\displaystyle|R(h)|\leqslant C\,\bigl[h^{5}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}+h^{6}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})}^{2}\bigr]\,\bigl[1+\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}\bigr]

and with the functions

\displaystyle g_{1}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\tanh(x/2)}{x^{2}}-\frac{1}{2x}\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}(x/2)},

\displaystyle g_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{2x}\frac{\tanh(x/2)}{\cosh^{2}(x/2)},

(3.19)

the coefficients
\Lambda_{0}
,
\Lambda_{1}
,
\Lambda_{2}
, and
\Lambda_{3}
in
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D,h}
are given by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\beta_{c}^{2}}{16}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;|(-2)\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}(p)|^{2}\;\bigl(g_{1}(\beta_{c}(p^{2}-\mu))+\frac{2}{3}\beta_{c}\,p^{2}\,g_{2}(\beta_{c}(p^{2}-\mu))\bigr),

(3.20)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}^{2}}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;|(-2)\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}(p)|^{2}\;g_{1}({\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}(p^{2}-\mu)),

(3.21)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\beta_{c}}{8}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\frac{|(-2)\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}(p)|^{2}}{\cosh^{2}(\frac{\beta_{c}}{2}(p^{2}-\mu))},

(3.22)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\beta_{c}^{2}}{16}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;|(-2)\widehat{V\alpha_{*}}(p)|^{4}\;\frac{g_{1}(\beta_{c}(p^{2}-\mu))}{p^{2}-\mu}.

(3.23)

It has been argued in
[
21
,
22
]
that the coefficients
\Lambda_{0}
,
\Lambda_{2}
, and
\Lambda_{3}
are positive. The coefficient
\Lambda_{1}
can, in principle, have either sign. Its sign is related to the derivative of
T_{\mathrm{c}}
with respect to
\mu
, see the remark below Eq. (1.21) in
[
21
]
.

We highlight the small factor
h^{5}
in front of the
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}
-norm of
\Psi
in the bound for
|R(h)|
. It is worse than the comparable estimate in
[
16
, Theorem 3.5]
, which is a consequence of the presence of the periodic vector potential
A
. The error is, however, of the same size as the related error terms in
[
21
,
22
]
.

Theorem
3.6
provides us with a result for the BCS energy for temperatures of the form
T={T_{\mathrm{c}}}(1-Dh^{2})
with
D\in\mathbb{R}
fixed. In the proof of Theorem
2
(a) we also need the information that our system is superconducting for smaller temperatures. The precise statement is captured in the following proposition.

Proposition 3.7

(A priori bound on Theorem
2
(a))
.

Let Assumptions
1.1
and
1.2
(a) hold and let
T_{0}>0
. Then, there are constants
h_{0}>0
and
D_{0}>0
such that for all
0<h\leqslant h_{0}
and all temperatures
T
obeying

\displaystyle T_{0}\leqslant T<{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(1-D_{0}h^{2}),

there is a BCS state
\Gamma
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma_{0})<0.

(3.24)

3.3
The upper bound on (
1.22
) and proof of Theorem
2
(a)

The results in the previous section can be used to prove the upper bound on (
1.22
) and Theorem
2
(a). These proof are almost literally the same as in the case of a constant magnetic field, and we therefore refer to
[
16
, Section 3.3]
for a detailed presentation.

4
Proofs of the Results in Section
3

4.1
Schatten norm estimates for operators given by product kernels

During our trial state analysis, we frequently need Schatten norm estimates for operators defined by integral kernels of the form
\tau(x-y)\Psi((x+y)/2)
. The relevant estimates are provided in the following lemma, whose proof can be found in
[
16
, Lemma 4.1]
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
h>0
, let
\Psi
be a gauge-periodic function on
Q_{h}
and let
\tau
be an even and real-valued function on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Moreover, let the operator
\alpha
be defined via its integral kernel
\alpha(X,r)\coloneqq\tau(r)\Psi(X)
, i.e.,
\alpha
acts as

\displaystyle\alpha f(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}y\;\tau(x-y)\Psi\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)f(y),

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(a)

Let
p\in\{2,4,6\}
. If
\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{p}(Q_{h})
and
\tau\in L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, then
\alpha\in\mathcal{S}^{p}
and

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{p}\leqslant C\;\|\tau\|_{\frac{p}{p-1}}\;\|\Psi\|_{p}.

(b)

For any
\nu>3
, there is a
C_{\nu}>0
, independent of
h
, such that if
(1+|\cdot|)^{\nu}\tau\in L^{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{6}(Q_{h})
, then
\alpha\in\mathcal{S}^{\infty}
and

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{\nu}\,h^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\;\max\{1,h^{\nu}\}\;\|(1+|\cdot|)^{\nu}\tau\|_{\nicefrac{{6}}{{5}}}\;\|\Psi\|_{6}.

4.2
Magnetic resolvent estimates

In this section we provide bounds for the resolvent kernel

\displaystyle G^{z}_{h}(x,y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{z-(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{h})^{2}+\mu}(x,y),

\displaystyle x,y

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

(4.1)

of the magnetic Laplacian that will be applied extensively in the proofs of Proposition
3.2
and Theorem
3.6
. Our analysis is based on gauge-invariant perturbation theory in the spirit of Nenciu, see
[
42
, Section V]
, and generalizes the analysis for the constant magnetic field in
[
19
, Section 2]
and
[
16
, Section 4.4.1]
. In case of a bounded magnetic vector potential, versions of some of our results appeared in
[
17
]
.

We introduce the non-integrable phase factor, also called the Wilson line, by

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)\coloneqq-\int_{y}^{x}\mathbf{A}(u)\cdot\mathrm{d}u\coloneqq-\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y))\cdot(x-y).

(4.2)

In case of the constant magnetic field the right side of (
4.2
) reduces to
\frac{\mathbb{B}}{2}\cdot(x\wedge y)
. We also define the gauge-invariant kernel
g_{h}^{z}(x,y)
via the equation

\displaystyle G_{h}^{z}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(x,y)}g_{h}^{z}(x,y),

\displaystyle x,y

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

(4.3)

It should be compared to the translation-invariant (and gauge-invariant) kernel
g_{B}^{z}(x-y)
introduced in
[
19
, Eq. (4.27)]
for the constant magnetic field. Its gauge-invariance makes it the natural starting point for a perturbative analysis.

The integral kernel of the operator
(z+\Delta+\mu)^{-1}
will be denoted by
g_{0}^{z}(x-y)
. The main result of this subsection is the following proposition.

Proposition 4.2
.

Assume that
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{e_{3}}+A
with
A\in W^{3,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
. For
t,\omega\in\mathbb{R}
let

\displaystyle f(t,\omega)\coloneqq\frac{|\omega|+|t+\mu|}{(|\omega|+(t+\mu)_{-})^{2}},

(4.4)

where
x_{-}\coloneqq-\min\{x,0\}
. For any
a\geqslant 0
, there are constants
\delta_{a},C_{a}>0
such that for all
t,\omega\in\mathbb{R}
and for all
h\geqslant 0
with
f(t,\omega)\,h^{2}\leqslant\delta_{a}
there are (
h
-dependent) even
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-functions
\rho^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}
,
\rho_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}
,
\tau^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}
, and
\tau_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}
such that

\displaystyle|g_{h}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\rho^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x-y),

\displaystyle|\nabla_{x}g_{h}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\rho_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x-y),

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}g_{h}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\rho_{\nabla}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x-y),

(4.5)

as well as

\displaystyle|g_{h}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x,y)-g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x-y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\tau^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x-y),

\displaystyle|\nabla_{x}g_{h}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x,y)-\nabla_{x}g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x-y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\tau_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x-y),

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}g_{h}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x,y)-\nabla_{y}g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x-y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\tau_{\nabla}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega+t}(x-y).

(4.6)

Furthermore, we have the estimates

\displaystyle\|\,|\cdot|^{a}\rho^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}\,f(t,\omega)^{1+\frac{a}{2}},

\displaystyle\|\,|\cdot|^{a}\rho_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}\,f(t,\omega)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{a}{2}}\,\Bigl[1+\frac{|\omega|+|t-\mu|}{|\omega|+(t-\mu)_{-}}\Bigr],

(4.7)

and

\displaystyle\|\,|\cdot|^{a}\tau^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}\,h^{3}\,f(t,\omega)^{\frac{5}{2}+\frac{a}{2}},

\displaystyle\|\,|\cdot|^{a}\tau_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}\,h^{3}\,f(t,\omega)^{2+\frac{a}{2}}\Bigl[1+\frac{|\omega|+|t-\mu|}{|\omega|+(t-\mu)_{-}}\Bigr].

(4.8)

Remark 4.3
.

The bounds in the above proposition should be compared to those for
g_{B}^{z}(x)
in
[
19
, Lemma 10]
(estimates without gradient) and
[
16
, Lemma 4.5]
(estimates with gradient). Although the kernel
g_{h}^{z}(x,y)
defined in (
4.3
) is not translation-invariant,
|g_{h}^{z}(x,y)|
,
|g_{h}^{z}(x,y)-g_{0}^{z}(x-y)|
, and the same terms with a gradient can be bounded by translation-invariant kernels. Moreover, these translation-invariant kernels satisfy
L^{1}
-norm bounds that are mostly of the same quality as those obtained for the kernels
|g_{B}^{z}(x)|
,
|g_{B}^{z}(x)-g_{0}^{z}(x)|
, and the same terms with a gradient in
[
19
,
16
]
. We highlight that, in comparison to
[
16
, Eq. (4.34)]
, we lose a power of the small parameter
h
in the estimate in (
4.8
). This is due to the second term in the bracket in (
4.20
) below and it is in accordance with comparable bounds in
[
21
]
and
[
22
]
. The fact that the above kernels can be bounded from above by translation-invariant kernels is an important ingredient for the proofs of Proposition
3.2
and Theorem
3.6
.

Before we give the proof of Proposition
4.2
we provide two lemmas. The first lemma concerns
L^{1}
-norm bounds for the kernel
g_{0}^{z}
and its gradient. Its proof can be found in
[
16
, Lemma 4.4]
. The bound for
g_{0}^{z}
(but not the one for
\nabla g_{0}^{z}
) appeared previously in
[
22
, Lemma 9]
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
a>-2
. There is a constant
C_{a}>0
such that for
t,\omega\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\,|\cdot|^{a}g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}\;f(t,\omega)^{1+\frac{a}{2}}

(4.9)

with
f(t,\omega)
in (
4.4
). Furthermore, for any
a>-1
, there is a constant
C_{a}>0
with

\displaystyle\left\|\,|\cdot|^{a}\nabla g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega+t}\right\|_{1}\leqslant C_{a}\;f(t,\omega)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{a}{2}}\;\Bigl[1+\frac{|\omega|+|t+\mu|}{|\omega|+(t+\mu)_{-}}\Bigr].

(4.10)

The second lemma provides us with formulas for the gradient of the function
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)
defined in (
4.2
) with respect to
x
and
y
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Assume that
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{e_{3}}+A
with
A\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Then we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{A}(x)+\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(x,y),

\displaystyle\nabla_{y}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}(y)-\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(y,x),

(4.11)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;t\curl\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y))\wedge(x-y)

(4.12)

is the transversal Poincaré gauge relative to
y
.

Remark 4.6
.

The function
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)
is a gauge transformation that relates
\mathbf{A}(x)
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)
.

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

From Morrey’s inequality we know that
\curl\mathbf{A}
is Lipschitz continuous, and hence the line integral in (
4.12
) is well defined. For two vector fields
v
and
w
we have

\displaystyle\nabla(v\cdot w)=(v\cdot\nabla)w+(w\cdot\nabla)v+v\wedge\curl w+w\wedge\curl v.

(4.13)

We apply this equality for fixed
y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
to

\displaystyle v(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}t\;\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y)),

\displaystyle w(x)

\displaystyle=x-y.

Our definition implies
\curl w=0
and we find that

\displaystyle-\nabla_{x}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y))\cdot\nabla_{x}\Bigr)(x-y)

\displaystyle\hskip-60.0pt+\left((x-y)\cdot\nabla_{x}\right)\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y))+(x-y)\wedge\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;t\,\curl\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y)).

(4.14)

The first term on the right side equals

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{3}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\mathbf{A}_{i}(y+t(x-y))\,\partial_{i}(x-y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y)).

(4.15)

To rewrite the second term on the right side of (
4.14
), we use integration by parts and find

\displaystyle\left((x-y)\cdot\nabla_{x}\right)\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y))

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;t\,\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y))

\displaystyle\hskip-50.0pt=t\,\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y))\Big|_{0}^{1}-\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y)).

(4.16)

Therefore, the sum of the terms in (
4.15
) and (
4.16
) equals
\mathbf{A}(x)
. Since the last term on the right side of (
4.14
) equals
-\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)
, this proves the first equation in (
4.11
). The second equation follows from
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)=-\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(y,x)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

We use the abbreviation
z=\mathrm{i}\omega+t
throughout the proof. In the first step we express
G_{h}^{z}(x,y)
in (
4.1
) in terms of the kernel

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{h}^{z}(x,y)\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(x,y)}\,g_{0}^{z}(x-y).

(4.17)

From (
4.11
) we know that

\displaystyle(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{x}+\mathbf{A}_{h}(x))\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(x,y)}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(x,y)}\;(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{x}+\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{h}(x,y)),

(4.18)

where
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)
in (
4.12
) is our vector potential in Poincaré gauge. Furthermore, a short computation shows that (
4.18
) implies the operator equation

\displaystyle(z-(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{h})^{2}+\mu)\widetilde{G}_{h}^{z}=\mathbbs 1-T_{h}^{z},

(4.19)

where
T_{h}^{z}
is the operator defined by the integral kernel

\displaystyle T_{h}^{z}(x,y)\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(x,y)}\bigl(2\,\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{h}(x,y)(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{x})-\mathrm{i}\divv_{x}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{h}(x,y)+|\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{h}(x,y)|^{2}\bigr)g_{0}^{z}(x-y).
