(4.20)

Since
g_{0}^{z}
is a radial function and the vector
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)
is perpendicular to
x-y
the first term on the right side vanishes. The operator
T_{h}^{z}
also appears in
[
42
]
.

We claim that

\displaystyle|T_{h}^{z}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant M_{\mathbf{A}}\,h^{3}\;\eta^{z}(x-y)

(4.21)

holds, where
\eta^{z}
is the (
h
-dependent) function

\displaystyle\eta^{z}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigl(|x|+\|\curl\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\,h\,|x|^{2}\bigr)\,|g_{0}^{z}(x)|

(4.22)

and

\displaystyle M_{\mathbf{A}}\coloneqq\max\bigl\{\|\curl(\curl\mathbf{A})\|_{\infty}\,,\,\|\curl\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\bigr\}.

(4.23)

We note that the bound in (
4.21
) holds as an equality with a similar function on the right side in the case of the constant magnetic field, see the proof of Lemma 10 in
[
19
]
.

To prove (
4.21
), we first derive a bound for
|\divv_{x}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)|
. For two vector fields
v
and
w
we have

\displaystyle\divv(v\wedge w)=\curl(v)\cdot w-\curl(w)\cdot v.

We apply the above equality with the choice
v(x)=\curl\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y))
,
w(x)=x-y
and use
\curl w=0
to write

\displaystyle\divv_{x}\bigl(\curl\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y))\wedge(x-y)\bigr)

\displaystyle=t\;\curl(\curl\mathbf{A})(y+t(x-y))\cdot(x-y).

Since
\curl(\curl\mathbf{A})
is Lipschitz continuous, which follows from
A\in W^{3,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we conclude that

\displaystyle|\divv_{x}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;t^{2}\,|\curl(\curl\mathbf{A})(y+t(x-y))\cdot(x-y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\curl(\curl\mathbf{A})\|_{\infty}\;|x-y|.

(4.24)

We also have

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;t\;|\curl\mathbf{A}(y+t(x-y))\wedge(x-y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\curl\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\,|x-y|.

(4.25)

In combination with
\|\curl(\curl\mathbf{A}_{h})\|_{\infty}\leqslant M_{\mathbf{A}}h^{3}
and
\|\curl\mathbf{A}_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2}\leqslant M_{\mathbf{A}}\,\|\curl A\|_{\infty}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}h^{4}
, this proves (
4.21
).

Next, we have a closer look at the function
\eta^{z}
. Lemma
4.4
and the assumption
f(t,\omega)\,h^{2}\leqslant\delta_{a}
imply the bound

\displaystyle\|\,|\cdot|^{a}\eta^{z}\|_{1}\leqslant C_{a}\;f(t,\omega)^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{a}{2}}\bigl[1+h\,f(t,\omega)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\bigr]\leqslant C_{a}\,f(t,\omega)^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{a}{2}}.

(4.26)

In particular,

\displaystyle M_{\mathbf{A}}\,h^{3}\,\|\eta^{z}\|_{1}\leqslant C\,M_{\mathbf{A}}\,h^{3}\,f(t,\omega)^{\frac{3}{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}

(4.27)

for all allowed
t,\omega
and
h
provided
\delta_{a}
is chosen small enough. The bound in (
4.21
) and an application of Young’s inequality therefore show that the operator norm of
T_{h}^{z}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|T_{h}^{z}\|_{\infty}\leqslant M_{\mathbf{A}}\,h^{3}\;\|\eta^{z}\|_{1}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}.

(4.28)

We use (
4.19
) and this bound to write the resolvent of the magnetic Laplacian as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{z-(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{h})^{2}+\mu}=\widetilde{G}_{h}^{z}\;\frac{1}{1-T_{h}^{z}}=\widetilde{G}_{h}^{z}\;\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\bigl(T_{h}^{z}\bigr)^{j}.

(4.29)

This finishes the first step of our proof. In the second step we use (
4.29
) to prove the claimed bounds for the integral kernel
g_{h}^{z}(x,y)
. Our first goal is to prove the bounds for
g_{h}^{z}(x,y)
without a gradient.

In the following we use the notation
\mathcal{S}_{h}^{z}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(T_{h}^{z})^{j}
. Eq. (
4.29
) allows us to write the kernel
g_{h}^{z}(x,y)
as

\displaystyle g_{h}^{z}(x,y)=g_{0}^{z}(x-y)+\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(x,y)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}u\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(x,u)}g_{0}^{z}(x-u)\;\mathcal{S}_{h}^{z}(u,y).

(4.30)

We use (
4.21
) to bound the integral kernel of the operator
\mathcal{S}_{h}^{z}
by

|\mathcal{S}_{h}^{z}(x,y)|\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(M_{\mathbf{A}}h^{3})^{j}(\eta^{z})^{*j}(x-y)\eqqcolon s^{z}(x-y),

(4.31)

where
(\eta^{z})^{*j}
denotes the
j
-fold convolution of
\eta^{z}
with itself. An application of the inequality

\displaystyle|x_{1}+\cdots+x_{j}|^{a}\leqslant j^{(a-1)_{+}}\bigl(|x_{1}|^{a}+\cdots+|x_{j}|^{a}\bigr)

(4.32)

with
a\geqslant 0
and
x_{+}=\max\{0,x\}
allows us to see that the function
s^{z}
satisfies the pointwise bound

\displaystyle|x|^{a}\,s^{z}(x)\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(M_{\mathbf{A}}h^{3})^{j}\sum_{m=1}^{j}j^{(a-1)_{+}}\,\eta^{z}*\cdots*\bigl(|\cdot|^{a}\eta^{z}\bigr)*\cdots*\eta^{z}(x).

Here,
|\cdot|^{a}\eta^{z}
appears in the
m
{}^{\textrm{th}}
slot. An application of (
4.26
), (
4.27
), and Young’s inequality therefore implies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}x\ |x|^{a}s^{z}(x)

\displaystyle\leqslant M_{\mathbf{A}}h^{3}\,\|\,|\cdot|^{a}\eta^{z}\|_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{j^{1+(a-1)_{+}}}{2^{j-1}}\leqslant C_{a}\,h^{3}\,f(t,\omega)^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{a}{2}}.

(4.33)

Let us also define the functions

\displaystyle\rho^{z}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq|g_{0}^{z}(x)|+|g_{0}^{z}|\ast s^{z}(x),

\displaystyle\tau^{z}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq|g_{0}^{z}|\ast s^{z}(x).

From (
4.30
) we know that

\displaystyle|g_{h}^{z}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\rho^{z}(x-y)

\displaystyle|g_{h}^{z}(x,y)-g_{0}^{z}(x-y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\tau^{z}(x-y).

The claimed bounds for the
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-norms of
\rho^{z}
and
\tau^{z}
in (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) follow from Lemma
4.4
, (
4.31
), and (
4.33
). It remains to prove the bounds involving a gradient.

An application of Lemma
4.5
and (
4.25
) show

\displaystyle|\nabla_{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,u)}|\leqslant|\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)|+|\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(x,u)|\leqslant Ch^{2}\left(|x-y|+|x-u|\right).

In combination with (
4.30
) and (
4.31
), this implies

|\nabla_{x}g_{h}^{z}(x,y)|\leqslant|\nabla g_{0}^{z}(x-y)|+Ch^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}u\left(|x-u|+|u-y|\right)|g_{0}^{z}(x-u)|s^{z}(u-y).

We denote the right side of the above equation by
\rho_{\nabla}^{z}(x-y)
. The claimed bound for
\rho_{\nabla}^{z}
follows immediately from those for
g_{0}^{z}
and
s^{z}
, see Lemma
4.4
and (
4.33
), and the assumption
f(t,\omega)\,h^{2}\leqslant\delta_{a}
. A bound for
|\nabla_{x}g_{h}^{z}(x,y)-\nabla g_{0}^{z}(x-y)|
can be obtained similarly. The bounds for
|\nabla_{y}g_{h}^{z}(x,y)|
and
|\nabla_{y}g_{h}^{z}(x,y)-\nabla g_{0}^{z}(x-y)|
can be obtained when we use the identity
G_{h}^{z}(x,y)=\overline{G_{h}^{\overline{z}}(y,x)}
. This proves Proposition
4.2
.
∎

4.3
Proof of Lemma
3.1

Let us recall the definition of
\Gamma_{\Delta}
in (
3.4
). From its definition we infer that it is a gauge-periodic generalized fermionic one-particle density matrix. Consequently, we only need to verify the trace class condition in (
1.8
).

We use the identity
(\exp(x)+1)^{-1}=(1-\tanh(x/2))/2
to write
\Gamma_{\Delta}
as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\Delta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\tanh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr).

(4.34)

Let us also recall the Mittag–Leffler series expansion

\displaystyle\tanh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}z\bigr)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-z},

(4.35)

see e.g.
[
16
, Eq. (3.12)]
. Its convergence becomes manifest by combining the
+n
and
-n
terms. When we use (
4.34
), and the resolvent identity

\displaystyle(z-T)^{-1}=(z-S)^{-1}+(z-T)^{-1}\;(S-T)\;(z-S)^{-1}

(4.36)

for two operators
S
and
T
, we find

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\Delta}=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\tanh\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}H_{\Delta}\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{\Delta}}=\Gamma_{0}+\mathcal{O}+\mathcal{Q}_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta).

(4.37)

Here
\Gamma_{0}
denotes the normal state in (
1.12
) and

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{0}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{0}}\delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{\Delta}}

(4.38)

with
\delta
in (
3.3
).

Since the diagonal components of the operator
\mathcal{O}
equal zero, this term does not contribute to the
11
-component
\gamma_{\Delta}
of
\Gamma_{\Delta}
. In the following, we use the notation
\pi=-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}
. To see that
(1+\pi^{2})[\mathcal{Q}_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)]_{11}
is locally trace class, we use

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}\pm H_{0}}\,\delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}\pm H_{0}}\,\delta

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}\pm\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\,\Delta\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}\mp\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}}\,\overline{\Delta}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}\mp\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}}\,\overline{\Delta}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}\pm\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\,\Delta\end{pmatrix}

to write it as

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathcal{Q}_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)\bigr]_{11}=\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}}\,\overline{\Delta}\,\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-H_{\Delta}}\Bigr]_{11}.

The operator
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}
is defined in (
3.2
). An application of Hölder’s inequality in (
2.1
) shows that the local trace norm of the term inside the sum is bounded by

\displaystyle\Bigl\|(1+\pi^{2})\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\Bigr\|_{\infty}\;\frac{1}{|\omega_{n}|^{2}}\;\|\Delta\|_{2}^{2}.

Using Cauchy-Schwarz, we see that
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}\leqslant C(1+\pi^{2})
, which implies that the operator norm in the above equation is bounded uniformly for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
|\omega_{n}|^{-2}
is summable in
n
and
\Delta
is locally Hilbert-Schmidt these considerations show that
(1+\pi^{2})[\mathcal{Q}_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)]_{11}
is locally trace class. It remains to show that
(1+\pi^{2})\gamma_{0}
with
\gamma_{0}
in (
1.12
) is locally trace class.

To that end, we first note that

\left\|(1+\pi^{2})\gamma_{0}\right\|_{1}\leqslant\left\|(1+\pi^{2})\frac{1}{1+\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}+\mu}\right\|_{\infty}\ \left\|(1+\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}+\mu)\gamma_{0}\right\|_{1}.

We argue as above to see that the first norm on the right side is finite. To obtain a bound for the second norm, we first note that there is a constant
C>0
such that
(1+x)(\exp(\beta(x-\mu))+1)^{-1}\leqslant C\exp(-\beta x/2)
holds for
x>a>-\infty
. The constant
C
depends on
\beta,\mu
, and
a
. Accordingly,

\left\|(1+\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}+\mu)\gamma_{0}\right\|_{1}\leqslant C\Tr\exp(-\beta\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}/2).

From Corollary A.1.2 and Corollary B.13.3 in
[
47
]
we know that for any
t>0
the operator
\exp(-t\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W})
has an integral kernel
k_{t}(x,y)
that satisfies

\left\|k_{t}\right\|_{2,\infty}^{2}=\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}y\ |k_{t}(x,y)|^{2}<\infty.

Accordingly,

\Tr\exp(-\beta\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}/2)=\left\|\exp(-\beta\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}/4)\right\|_{2}^{2}=\fint_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}y\ |k_{\beta/4}(x,y)|^{2}\leqslant\|k_{\beta/4}\|_{2,\infty}^{2}.

We conclude that
(1+\pi^{2})\gamma_{0}
is locally trace class. This ends the proof of Lemma
3.1
.

4.4
Proof of Lemma
3.4

We start by proving that
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
is a bounded linear operator on
{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
. To that end, we first check that for
\Delta\in{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
, we have
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}\Delta\in\mathcal{S}^{2}
. Using Hölder’s inequality for the trace per unit volume in (
2.1
) and
\omega_{n}=\pi(2n+1)T
, we see that

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\|\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}}\right\|_{2}\leqslant\|\Delta\|_{2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\omega_{n}^{2}}\leqslant C\|\Delta\|_{2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{(2n+1)^{2}},

(4.39)

which proves the claim.

Next, we show that
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}\Delta
satisfies (
2.10
). To that end, we need the identity

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}-\mu}(x,y)=-\frac{1}{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}+\mu}(y,x).

(4.40)

It follows from
\overline{\mathcal{R}^{*}(x,y)}=\mathcal{R}(y,x)
for a general operator
\mathcal{R}
with kernel
\mathcal{R}(x,y)
and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{z-\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}+\mu}=\overline{\Bigl(\frac{1}{z-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}+\mu}\Bigr)^{*}}.

Using the coordinate transformation
(w_{1},w_{2})\mapsto(w_{1}+v,w_{2}+v)
and the above identities for the resolvent kernel, we see that

\displaystyle L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}\Delta(X+v,r)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}w_{1}\mathrm{d}w_{2}\;\Delta\bigl(\frac{w_{1}+w_{2}}{2}+v,w_{1}-w_{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip-60.0pt\times\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}+\mu}\bigl(X+v+\frac{r}{2},w_{1}\bigr)\frac{1}{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}+\mu}\bigl(X+v-\frac{r}{2},w_{2}\bigr)

(4.41)

holds. We highlight that we wrote
\Delta
in terms of relative and center-of-mass coordinates in (
4.41
). We have
T(v)\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}T(v)^{*}=\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}
,
v\in\Lambda_{h}
, where
T(v)
is the magnetic translation in (
1.3
), and hence the same identity with
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}
replaced by its resolvent. In combination with

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\;\overline{\varphi(x)}\,[T(v)(z-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W})^{-1}T(v)^{*}](x,y)\,\varphi(y)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\;\overline{\varphi(x)}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(v\wedge(x-y))}\,(z-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W})^{-1}(x+v,y+v)\,\varphi(y),

which holds for
z\in\rho(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W})
, this proves that the resolvent kernel of
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}
obeys the first relation in (
1.7
). When we combine this and the fact that
\Delta
satisfies the second relation in (
1.7
), we see that we pick up the total phase

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(v\wedge(X+\frac{r}{2}-w_{1}))}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(v\wedge(X-\frac{r}{2}-w_{2}))}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}\cdot(v\wedge(w_{1}+w_{2}))}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(v\wedge X)}

in (
4.41
). This proves the first equation in (
2.10
) for
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}\Delta(X,r)
.

To see that the second equation in (
2.10
) is true, we further observe that the Matsubara frequencies obey
-\omega_{n}=\omega_{-(n+1)}
. Hence, the index shift
n\mapsto-n-1
and (
4.40
) imply the desired symmetry. This proves that
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
is a bounded linear operator on
{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
.

To show that
N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)\in\mathcal{S}^{2}
, we use the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\|\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}}\,\overline{\Delta}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt\leqslant C\,\|\Delta\|_{2}\ \|\Delta\|_{\infty}^{2}\ \sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{(2n+1)^{4}}.

The proof of (
2.10
) for
N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)
goes along the same lines as that for
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}\Delta
. This proves Lemma
3.4
.

4.5
Proof of Theorem
3.6

Before we start with the proof of Theorem
3.6
, we briefly mention the two main steps. In the first step we compute
\langle\Delta,L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}\Delta\rangle
. To that end, we decompose the operator
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
into several increasingly simpler parts, which allows us to extract the quadratic terms in the GL functional. The related analysis can be found in Sections
4.5.1
–
4.5.8
. The quartic term in the GL functional emerges from
\langle\Delta,N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}(\Delta)\rangle
. In the second step we study the nonlinear operator
N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
and introduce comparable steps of simplification as for
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
. The related analysis starts in Section
4.5.9
. The relation to the existing literature will be discussed mostly in Sections
4.5.2
,
4.5.6
, and
4.5.9
after the relevant mathematical objects have been introduced.

4.5.1
Decomposition of
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
— separation of
W

We use the resolvent equation in (
4.36
) to decompose the operator
L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
in (
3.11
) as

\displaystyle L_{T,\mathbf{A},W}=L_{T,\mathbf{A}}+\mathcal{W}_{T,\mathbf{A}}+\mathcal{R}_{T,\mathbf{A},W}^{(2)},

(4.42)

where
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}=L_{T,\mathbf{A},0}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}\,W_{h}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}}\,W_{h}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}}\Bigr],

(4.43)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{T,\mathbf{A},W}^{(2)}\Delta

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Bigl[\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}\,W_{h}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}\,W_{h}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 60.0pt+\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}}\,W_{h}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}}\,W_{h}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 60.0pt-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}\,W_{h}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}-\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A},W}}}\,W_{h}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}+\overline{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{A}}}}\Bigr].

(4.44)

In the special case of a constant magnetic field, the operator
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}
appeared for the first time in
[
19
]
. The operator
\mathcal{W}_{T,0}
(case of no external magnetic fields) was studied in
[
18
]
. In the sections
4.5.2
–
4.5.4
we analyze
\langle\Delta,L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\rangle
and extract the first and the third term in the GL functional from it. Afterwards, we study in Sections
4.5.5
and
4.5.6
the quadratic form
\langle\Delta,\mathcal{W}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\rangle
, which contributes the second term of the GL functional. Finally, in Section
4.5.8
, we collect the results of the previous sections and provide a bound for
\langle\Delta,\mathcal{R}_{T,\mathbf{A},W}^{(2)}\Delta\rangle
showing that this term does not contribute to the GL functional.

4.5.2
A representation formula for
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}
and an outlook on the quadratic terms

In the following subsections we compute the contribution from
\langle\Delta,L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\rangle
to the Ginzburg–Landau energy. Our analysis is based on the following representation formula for the operator
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}
, which characterizes its action solely in terms of relative and center-of-mass coordinates.

Lemma 4.7
.

The operator
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\colon{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}\rightarrow{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
acts as

\displaystyle(L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\alpha)(X,r)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T,\mathbf{A}}(X,Z,r,s)\;(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\alpha)(X,s)

with

\displaystyle k_{T,\mathbf{A}}(X,Z,r,s)\coloneqq\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}k_{T,\mathbf{A}}^{n}(X,Z,r,s)\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(X,Z,r,s)},

(4.45)

where

\displaystyle k_{T,\mathbf{A}}^{n}(X,Z,r,s)

\displaystyle\coloneqq g_{h}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(X+\frac{r}{2},X+Z+\frac{s}{2}\bigr)\;g_{h}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(X-\frac{r}{2},X+Z-\frac{s}{2}\bigr)

(4.46)

with
g_{h}^{z}
in (
4.3
) and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,Z,r,s)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\bigl(X+\frac{r}{2},X+Z+\frac{s}{2}\bigr)+\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\bigl(X-\frac{r}{2},X+Z-\frac{s}{2}\bigr)

(4.47)

with
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
in (
4.2
).

Remark 4.8
.

Lemma
4.7
should be compared to the representation formula in
[
19
, Lemma 11]
for the operator
L_{T,B}
in
[
19
, Eq. (8)]
. The differences between the two representation formulas are related to the fact that our magnetic field is non-constant. Accordingly, the kernel
g_{h}^{z}(x,y)
is not translation-invariant and
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(x,y)
does not simply equal
\frac{\mathbb{B}}{2}\cdot(x\wedge y)
. This results in the dependence of the function
k_{T,\mathbf{A}}^{n}
on the coordinate
X
and in the fact that the representation formula in Lemma
4.7
is not symmetric under the transformation
Z\mapsto-Z
. As a consequence, the operator
\cos(Z\cdot\Pi)
in
[
19
, Lemma 11]
is replaced by
\exp(\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X}))
. Both the cosine function and the full magnetic momentum operator in its argument, will be recovered at a later stage, see (
4.76
) below. The main guiding principle behind the definition of the above representation formula and its subsequent analysis is that we should think of
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(x,y)
as a generalization of
\frac{\mathbb{B}}{2}\cdot(x\wedge y)
. The latter has more convenient algebraic properties, which is responsible for much of the simplicity of the analysis in
[
19
,
16
]
in comparison to the present work. In case of
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(x,y)
we do computations as if similar relations were satisfied and afterwards carefully bound the emergent remainder terms.

Proof of Lemma
4.7
.

With (
4.40
) applied to
W=0
the integral kernel of
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}
can be written as

\displaystyle L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\alpha(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v\;G_{h}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(x,u)\,G_{h}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(y,v)\,\alpha(u,v).

We note that
\alpha
and
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\alpha
are not yet written in terms of relative and center-of-mass coordinates, which will be done in the next step. To that end, we define the coordinates
X=\frac{x+y}{2}
,
r=x-y
,

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=X+Z+\frac{s}{2},

\displaystyle v=X+Z-\frac{s}{2},

(4.48)

and introduce the notation
\zeta_{X}^{r}\coloneqq X+\frac{r}{2}
. This allows us to write the above equation as

\displaystyle L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;G_{h}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(\zeta_{X}^{r},\zeta_{X+Z}^{s})\,G_{h}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}(\zeta_{X}^{-r},\zeta_{X+Z}^{-s})\,\alpha(X+Z,s).

We highlight that, by a slight abuse of notation, we denoted the kernels of
\alpha
and
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\alpha
when expressed in terms of relative and center-of-mass coordinates still by the same symbols. When we use the relation
G^{z}_{h}(x,y)=\exp(\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,y))g_{h}^{z}(x,y)
and

\displaystyle\alpha(X+Z,s)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\alpha(X,s),

we see that the above identity implies the claimed formula.
∎

We analyze the operator
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}
in four steps. In the first three steps, we introduce three operators of increasing simplicity in their dependence on
\mathbf{A}_{h}
. More precisely, we write it as

\displaystyle L_{T,\mathbf{A}}=(L_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}})+(\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}})+(\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}-M_{T,\mathbf{A}})+M_{T,\mathbf{A}}

(4.49)

with the operators
\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
,
\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
, and
M_{T,\mathbf{A}}
defined below in (
4.50
), (
4.76
), and (
4.108
), respectively. As we will show, the operators in brackets in (
4.49
) do not contribute to the GL functional. The operator
\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
is obtained from
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}
when we replace the kernels
g_{h}^{z}(x,y)
in (
4.46
) by
g_{0}^{z}(x-y)
. To obtain the operator
\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
from
\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
, we need to replace the phase factor in the definition of
k_{T,\mathbf{A}}^{n}(X,Z,r,s)
by
\exp(-\mathrm{i}(r-s)\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)(r+s)/4)
, where
D\mathbf{A}_{h}
denotes the Jacobi matrix of
\mathbf{A}_{h}
, and
\exp(\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X}))
by
\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}})
. Finally,
M_{T,\mathbf{A}}
emerges when we replace
\exp(-\mathrm{i}(r-s)\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)(r+s)/4)
in the definition of
\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
by
1
. In the fourth and final step we extract the quadratic terms in the GL functional (except the one proportional to
W
) as well as a term that cancels the last term on the left side of (
3.18
) from
\langle\Delta,M_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\rangle
. To that end, we expand the operator
\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}})
up to second order in powers
Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}
and use
T=T_{\mathrm{c}}(1-Dh^{2})
.

In the case of a constant magnetic field a similar decomposition of
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}
has been introduced for the first time in
[
19
]
. In this reference the operator
\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
is called
M_{T,\mathbf{A}}
and our
M_{T,\mathbf{A}}
is called
N_{T,\mathbf{B}}
. We did not follow the notation in
[
19
]
because the symbol
N_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
is reserved for the nonlinear term in our paper. The decomposition of
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}
in
[
19
]
has also been used in
[
16
]
. In comparison to these two references we have an additional term (the operator
\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
) in our decomposition of
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}
, which is a consequence of the fact that we are dealing with a general magnetic field. Generally speaking, the magnetic vector potential
\mathbf{A}_{h}
is more difficult to treat than
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}
because several algebraic relations that hold for the latter do not hold for the former. Our main contribution in this section is that we overcome the related mathematical difficulties in the computation of the above terms. Another main difference between our work and
[
19
]
is that we additionally need
H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})
-norm estimates. In the special case of a constant magnetic field such bounds have been proved in
[
16
]
. It should also be noted that
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}
acts on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})
in
[
19
]
, while it acts on
{L^{2}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}
in our case and in
[
16
]
.

4.5.3
Approximation of
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}

4.5.3.1
The operator
\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
.

We define the operator
\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}}(X,Z,r,s)\;(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\alpha)(X,s)

(4.50)

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}}(X,Z,r,s)\coloneqq\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}k_{T}^{n}(Z,r-s)\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(X,Z,r,s)},

(4.51)

where
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}}
is defined in (
4.47
) and

\displaystyle k_{T}^{n}(Z,r)\coloneqq k_{T,0}^{n}(0,Z,r,0)=g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\,g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z+\frac{r}{2}\bigr).

(4.52)

The following proposition allows us to replace the operator
L_{T,\mathbf{A}}
by
\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
in the computation of the BCS energy of our trial state.

Proposition 4.9
.

Assume that
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{e_{3}}+A
with
A\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
periodic, let
|\cdot|^{k}V\alpha_{*}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for
k\in\{0,1\}
,
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})
, and denote
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
). For any
T_{0}>0
there is
h_{0}>0
such that for any
0<h\leqslant h_{0}
and any
T\geqslant T_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\|L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\|_{H^{1}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}^{2}\leqslant C\;h^{8}\;\left(\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\ |\cdot|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}.

Remark 4.10
.

In order to prove Theorem
3.6
, we only need the bound

|\langle\Delta,(L_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}})\Delta\rangle|\leqslant C\;h^{5}\;\left(\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\ |\cdot|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2},

which is a direct consequence of Cauchy–Schwarz, Proposition
4.9
, Lemma
4.1
, and (
2.7
). We prove the more general statement in Proposition
4.9
here because we need it in the proof of Proposition
3.2
in Section
4.6
below.

Let us define the functions

\displaystyle F_{T,h}^{a}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{m=0}^{a}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigl(|\cdot|^{m}\,\tau^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigr)*\rho^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}+\tau^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}*\bigl(|\cdot|^{m}\,\rho^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt+\bigl(|\cdot|^{a}\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*\tau^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\bigl(|\cdot|^{a}\,\tau^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigr)

(4.53)

with
a\geqslant 0
and

\displaystyle G_{T,h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{\#\in\{\pm\}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\tau_{\nabla}^{\#\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}*\rho^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}+\tau^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}*\rho_{\nabla}^{-\#\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}+|\nabla g_{0}^{\#\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\tau^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|*\tau_{\nabla}^{-\#\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}},

(4.54)

which play a prominent role in the proof of Proposition
4.9
. We also recall the definition of the Matsubara frequencies
\omega_{n}
in (
3.10
), of
g_{0}^{z}
in (
4.3
), and of the functions
\rho^{z}
,
\tau^{z}
,
\rho_{\nabla}^{z}
, and
\tau_{\nabla}^{z}
in Proposition
4.2
.

We claim that for any
T_{0}>0
there is
h_{0}>0
such that for any
0<h\leqslant h_{0}
and any
T\geqslant T_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\|F_{T,h}^{a}\|_{1}+\|G_{T,h}\|_{1}\leqslant C_{a}\;h^{3}.

(4.55)

To prove this claim, we apply Young’s inequality, Proposition
4.2
, and Lemma
4.4
, and note that the function
f(t,\omega)
in (
4.4
) obeys the estimate

\displaystyle f(0,\omega_{n})

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;|2n+1|^{-1}.

(4.56)

Moreover,

\displaystyle 1+\frac{|\omega_{n}|+|\mu|}{|\omega_{n}|+\mu_{-}}\leqslant C.

(4.57)

In combination, these considerations prove our claim. We are now prepared to give the proof of the above proposition.

Proof of Proposition
4.9
.

We have

\displaystyle\|L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\|_{H^{1}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-50.0pt+\|\Pi(L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}+\|\widetilde{\pi}(L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

(4.58)

and we claim that the first term on the right side satisfies

\displaystyle\|L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\;\|F_{T,h}^{0}*|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}.

(4.59)

Using Young’s inequality, (
2.7
), and (
4.55
), we see that the right side of (
4.59
) is bounded by a constant times
h^{8}\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}
. If (
4.59
) holds, this therefore proves the claimed bound for this term.

To prove (
4.59
), we start by noting that

\displaystyle\|L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}Z^{\prime}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\;|V\alpha_{*}(s)|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\hskip 60.0pt\times\esssup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|(k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}})(X,Z,r,s)|

\displaystyle\hskip 60.0pt\times\esssup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|(k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}})(X,Z^{\prime},r,s^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt\times\fint_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\Psi(X)|\;|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z^{\prime}\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\Psi(X)|.

(4.60)

Since the norm of the operator
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}
equals
1
, we have

\displaystyle\fint_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\Psi(X)|\;|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z^{\prime}\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\Psi(X)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}.

(4.61)

Consequently, (
4.60
) yields

\displaystyle\|L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-80.0pt\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\,\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;\esssup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|(k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}})(X,Z,r,s)|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s)|\Bigr|^{2}.

(4.62)

We note that

\displaystyle k_{T,\mathbf{A}}^{n}(X,Z,r,s)-k_{T}^{n}(Z,r-s)

\displaystyle\hskip-120.0pt=\bigl(g_{h}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigr)\bigl(X+\frac{r}{2},X+Z+\frac{s}{2}\bigr)\,g_{h}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(X-\frac{r}{2},X+Z-\frac{s}{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt+g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z-\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr)\,\bigl(g_{h}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}-g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigr)\bigl(X-\frac{r}{2},X+Z-\frac{s}{2}\bigr)

(4.63)

and hence, by Proposition
4.2
, the integrand in (
4.62
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\bigl|(k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}})(X,Z,r,s)\bigr|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2}{\beta}\,\smash{\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\,\bigl[\tau^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z-\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr)\;\rho^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z+\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt+|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigl(Z-\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr)\;\tau^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z+\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr)\bigr].

(4.64)

We combine (
4.64
) and the fact that the functions in (
4.64
) are even (see Proposition
4.2
), to show

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;\esssup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|(k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}})(X,Z,r,s)|\leqslant F_{T,h}^{0}(r-s),

(4.65)

where
F_{T,\mathbf{A}}^{0}
is the function in (
4.53
). When we apply (
4.65
) to (
4.62
), we obtain (
4.59
).

Let us pause for a moment and highlight the main idea behind the above bound because it will reappear frequently in the subsequent analysis. The kernels in (
4.63
) are not translation-invariant. From Proposition
4.2
we know, however, that they can by bounded by translation-invariant kernels. When we do this, we see that we obtain convolutions of translation-invariant kernels after the integration over
Z
has been carried out. These convolutions can now be estimated with the
L^{1}
-norm bounds in Proposition
4.2
. We highlight that this emergent simplicity is difficult to find when working in the operator picture. Our analysis is inspired by the analysis for the constant magnetic field in
[
19
]
, where much of the above structure is more apparent.

For the second term on the right side of (
4.58
), we claim the bound

\displaystyle\|\Pi(L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,h^{2}\;\|(F_{T,h}^{1}+G_{T,h})*|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,h^{8}\;\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}.

(4.66)

The second inequality follows from Young’s inequality and (
4.55
). To prove the first inequality in (
4.66
), we note that the gradient can act either on
\Psi
or on
k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
and we start by considering the term, where it acts on
\Psi
. We therefore apply (
4.60
) with
\exp(\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X}))
replaced by
\Pi_{X}\,\exp(\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X}))
(we recall that
\Pi_{X}=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X}+2\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}(X)
) and replace (
4.61
) by

\displaystyle\fint_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\;|\Pi_{X}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\Psi(X)|\;|\Pi_{X}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z^{\prime}\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\Psi(X)|

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\leqslant\|\Pi_{X}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\Psi\|_{2}\;\|\Pi_{X}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z^{\prime}\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\Psi\|_{2}.

From
a direct computation, we know that

\displaystyle\Pi_{X}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\bigl[\Pi_{X}-\mathbf{B}\wedge Z\bigr].

(4.67)

Using this and (
2.7
), we find

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{X}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X})}\Psi\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\Pi\Psi\|_{2}+|\mathbf{B}|\,|Z|\;\|\Psi\|_{2}\leqslant C\,h^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}\;(1+|Z|),

(4.68)

which subsequently proves

\displaystyle\|\Pi(L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,h^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-110.0pt\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\Bigl(\;\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;h\,(1+|Z|)\;\esssup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|(k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}})(X,Z,r,s)||V\alpha_{*}(s)|\Bigr|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-71.13188pt+\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;\esssup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|(\nabla_{X}k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\nabla_{X}\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}})(X,Z,r,s)|\,|V\alpha_{*}(s)|\Bigr|^{2}\;\Bigr).

(4.69)

We claim that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{X}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,Z,r,s)|\leqslant C\,\|D\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\Bigl(\bigl|Z+\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr|+\bigl|Z-\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr|\Bigr)

(4.70)

holds, where
D\mathbf{A}
denotes the Jacobi matrix of
\mathbf{A}
. To see this, we use Lemma
4.5
and compute

\displaystyle\nabla_{X}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,Z,r,s)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}\bigl(X+Z+\frac{s}{2}\bigr)-\mathbf{A}\bigl(X+\frac{r}{2}\bigr)+\mathbf{A}\bigl(X+Z-\frac{s}{2}\bigr)-\mathbf{A}\bigl(X-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt+\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}\bigl(X+\frac{r}{2},X+Z+\frac{s}{2}\bigr)-\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}\bigl(X+Z+\frac{s}{2},X+\frac{r}{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt+\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}\bigl(X-\frac{r}{2},X+Z-\frac{s}{2}\bigr)-\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}\bigl(X+Z-\frac{s}{2},X-\frac{r}{2}\bigr).

The claim is a direct computation of this equality and a first order Taylor approximation. In combination, Proposition
4.2
, (
4.63
), and (
4.70
) imply that, for
h
small enough,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;\esssup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|(\nabla_{X}k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\nabla_{X}\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}})(X,Z,r,s)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\bigl(G_{T,h}+F_{T,h}^{1}\bigr)(r-s).

The functions
F_{T,h}^{1}
and
G_{T,h}
are defined in (
4.53
) and (
4.53
), respectively. A similar argument that uses
|Z|\leqslant|Z+\frac{r}{2}|+|Z-\frac{r}{2}|
shows

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;(1+|Z|)\;\esssup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|(k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}})(X,Z,r,s)|\leqslant F_{T,h}^{1}(r-s).

When we insert these two bounds into (
4.69
), this proves (
4.66
). It remains to consider the third term on the right side of (
4.58
).

We claim that it satisfies

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\pi}(L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\;\|(F_{T,h}^{1}+G_{T,h})*|\ |\cdot|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,h^{8}\;\|\ |\cdot|\ V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}.

(4.71)

The second inequality follows from Young’s inequality, (
2.7
), and (
4.55
). To see that the first inequality holds, we first estimate

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\pi}(L_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\,\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;\esssup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|(\widetilde{\pi}k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{\pi}\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}})(X,Z,r,s)|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s)|\Bigr|^{2}.

(4.72)

We start by noting that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}k_{T,\mathbf{A}}(X,Z,r,s)-\widetilde{\pi}\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}}(X,Z,r,s)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,Z,r,s)}\bigl(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}+\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{B}\wedge r+\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,Z,r,s)\bigr)\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(k_{T,\mathbf{A}}^{n}(X,Z,r,s)-\widetilde{k}_{T}^{n}(Z,r-s))

and

\displaystyle\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,Z,r,s)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\bigl[\mathbf{A}\bigl(X+\frac{r}{2}\bigr)-\mathbf{A}\bigl(X-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\bigl[\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}\bigl(X+\frac{r}{2},X+Z+\frac{s}{2}\bigr)-\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}\bigl(X-\frac{r}{2},X+Z-\frac{s}{2}\bigr)\bigr],

which follows from Lemma
4.5
. We combine these two identities and estimate

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\pi}k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{\pi}\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}}|\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|\nabla_{r}k_{T,\mathbf{A}}^{n}-\nabla_{r}k_{T}^{n}|+|k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}}|\left(\frac{|\mathbf{B}|}{4}+h^{2}\|D\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\times\left(|r-s|+|s|+\left|Z+\frac{r-s}{2}\right|+\left|Z-\frac{r-s}{2}\right|\right).

(4.73)

We apply

\displaystyle|r-s|^{a}

\displaystyle=\bigl|\frac{r-s}{2}+Z+\frac{r-s}{2}-Z\bigr|^{a}\leqslant 2^{(a-1)_{+}}\Bigl(\bigl|Z-\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr|^{a}+\bigl|Z+\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr|^{a}\Bigr),

(4.74)

which holds for
a\geqslant 0
, with
a=1
to bound
|r-s|
in (
4.73
). When we put (
4.73
) and (
4.74
) together, and additionally use Lemma
4.2
, (
4.63
) and (
4.73
), we find

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;\esssup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|(\widetilde{\pi}k_{T,\mathbf{A}}-\widetilde{\pi}\widetilde{k}_{T,\mathbf{A}})(X,Z,r,s)|\leqslant C\bigl(F_{T,h}^{1}+G_{T,h}\bigr)(r-s)(1+|s|).

(4.75)

Finally, (
4.75
) and (
4.72
) imply (
4.71
), which finishes the proof of Proposition
4.9
.
∎

4.5.3.2
The operator
\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
.

We define
\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\alpha(X,r)\coloneqq\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)(r+s)}\,(\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}})\alpha)(X,s),

(4.76)

where

k_{T}(Z,r)\coloneqq\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z-\frac{r}{2}\bigr)\,g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}\bigl(Z+\frac{r}{2}\bigr).

(4.77)

By
(D\mathbf{A})_{ij}\coloneqq\partial_{j}\mathbf{A}_{i}
we denote the Jacobi matrix of
\mathbf{A}
. Here and in the following we use the notation that
D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)(r+s)
denotes the matrix
D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)
applied to
r+s
. Accordingly,
\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)(r+s)
is the inner product of this vector with
\frac{r-s}{4}
.

The following proposition allows us to replace
\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
by
\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
in our computations.

Proposition 4.11
.

Assume that
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{e_{3}}+A
with a periodic vector potential
A\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
, let
|\cdot|^{k}V\alpha_{*}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for
k\in\{0,1,2\}
,
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})
, and denote
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
). For any
T_{0}>0
there is
h_{0}>0
such that for any
0<h\leqslant h_{0}
and any
T\geqslant T_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\|_{H^{1}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;h^{8}\;\max_{k=0,1,2}\|\,|\cdot|^{k}V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}.

(4.78)

Before we give the proof of Proposition
4.11
, we state and prove two preparatory lemmas. The first lemma allows us to extract the term
\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X+Z)
and to replace
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(-\mathrm{i}\nabla)}
by
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}}
in the definition of
\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
. In
[
19
,
16
]
, where only the constant magnetic field is present, this approximation holds as an algebraic identity. A version of the first bound in (
4.79
) has, in case of a periodic vector potential, been proved in
[
17
]
. In this reference
Z
does not appear on the right side, which is why this bound appears to be wrong.

Lemma 4.12
.

Assume that
A\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
. We then have

\displaystyle\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)-\Phi_{2A}(X,X+Z)+\frac{1}{4}(r-s)\cdot DA(X)(r+s)\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip-200.0pt\leqslant C\;\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\;\bigl(|Z|+|r-s|\bigr)\bigl(|s|^{2}+|r-s|^{2}\bigr),

(4.79)

where
\Phi_{A}
is defined in (
4.2
) and
\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}
is defined in (
4.47
). We also have

\displaystyle\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\nabla_{X}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)-\nabla_{X}\Phi_{2A}(X,X+Z)\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt\leqslant C\;\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\;\bigl(|Z|+|r-s|\bigr)\bigl(|s|+|r-s|\bigr)

(4.80)

as well as

\displaystyle\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\nabla_{X}(r-s)\cdot DA(X)\cdot(r+s)\bigr|\leqslant C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\,|r-s|\,\bigl(|s|+|r-s|\bigr)

(4.81)

and

\displaystyle\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)+\frac{1}{4}\nabla_{r}(r-s)\cdot DA(X)(r+s)\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt\leqslant C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\,\bigl(|s|^{2}+|r-s|^{2}+|Z|^{2}\bigr).

(4.82)

The same bounds hold if
A
is replaced by
\mathbf{A}_{e_{3}}+A
.

Proof of Lemma
4.12
.

We use the notation
\zeta_{X}^{r}\coloneqq X+\frac{r}{2}
and start by writing

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\bigl[A\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{s+t(r-s)}\bigr)+A\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{-s-t(r-s)}\bigr)\bigr]\cdot Z

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt-\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\bigl[A\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{s+t(r-s)}\bigr)-A\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{-s-t(r-s)}\bigr)\bigr]\cdot\frac{r-s}{2}.

(4.83)

A second order Taylor expansion in the variable
\pm\frac{1}{2}(s+t(r-s))
allows us to show that

\displaystyle\Bigl|A\bigl(X+Z-tZ\pm\frac{s+t(r-s)}{2}\bigr)-A(X+Z-tZ)\mp\frac{1}{2}DA(X+Z-tZ)(s+t(r-s))\Bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip-220.0pt\leqslant C\;\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\;\bigl(|s|^{2}+|r-s|^{2}\bigr).

(4.84)

For the first term on the right side of (
4.83
), this implies

\displaystyle\Bigl|\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\bigl[A\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{s+t(r-s)}\bigr)+A\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{-s-t(r-s)}\bigr)\bigr]\cdot Z-\Phi_{2A}(X,X+Z)\Bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt\leqslant C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\,|Z|\,\bigl(|s|^{2}+|r-s|^{2}\bigr),

(4.85)

and we find

\displaystyle\Bigl|-\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\bigl[A\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{s+t(r-s)}\bigr)-A\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{-s-t(r-s)}\bigr)\bigr]\cdot\frac{r-s}{2}+\frac{1}{4}(r-s)\cdot DA(X)(r+s)\Bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip-350.0pt\leqslant\Bigl|-\frac{r-s}{2}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\bigl[DA(X+Z-tZ)-DA(X)\bigr](s+t(r-s))\Bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip-150.0pt+C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\,\bigl(|s|^{2}+|r-s|^{2}\bigr)\,|r-s|

\displaystyle\hskip-350.0pt\leqslant C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\,|r-s|\,\bigl[|s|^{2}+|r-s|^{2}+\bigl(|s|+|r-s|\bigr)|Z|\bigr]

(4.86)

for the second term. Adding up (
4.85
) and (
4.86
) proves (
4.79
). The proof of (
4.80
) results from a first order Taylor expansion and that of (
4.81
) is a straightforward computation. It remains to prove (
4.82
).

When we differentiate (
4.83
) with respect to
r
this yields

\displaystyle\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\frac{t}{2}\ Z\cdot\bigl[DA\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{s+t(r-s)}\bigr)-DA\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{-s-t(r-s)}\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt-\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\frac{t}{2}\ \frac{r-s}{2}\cdot\bigl[DA\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{s+t(r-s)}\bigr)+DA\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{-s-t(r-s)}\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\bigl[A\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{s+t(r-s)}\bigr)-A\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{-s-t(r-s)}\bigr)\bigr].

(4.87)

A first order Taylor expansion shows that the absolute value of the first term on the right side of (
4.87
) is bounded by
C\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}(|s|+|r-s|)|Z|
. We also note that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\nabla_{r}(r-s)\cdot DA(X)(r+s)=\frac{1}{4}(r-s)\cdot DA(X)+\frac{1}{4}DA(X)(r+s).

(4.88)

The second term on the right side of (
4.87
) obeys

\displaystyle\Bigl|\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;t\;\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot\bigl[DA\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{s+t(r-s)}\bigr)+DA\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{-s-t(r-s)}\bigr)\bigr]-\frac{1}{4}(r-s)\cdot DA(X)\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip-360.0pt\leqslant\Bigl|\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\frac{t}{2}\;(r-s)\cdot\bigl[DA(X+Z-tZ)-DA(X)\bigr]\Bigr|+C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\bigl(|s|+|r-s|\bigr)|r-s|

\displaystyle\hskip-360.0pt\leqslant C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\,\bigl[|r-s|\,|Z|+\bigl(|s|+|r-s|\bigr)|r-s|\bigr].

(4.89)

For the third term on the right side of (
4.87
), we use the bound

\displaystyle\Bigl|\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\bigl[A\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{s+t(r-s)}\bigr)-A\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{-s-t(r-s)}\bigr)\bigr]-\frac{1}{4}DA(X)(r+s)\Bigr|\leqslant\mathcal{T}_{+}+\mathcal{T}_{-}+\mathcal{T},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\pm}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigl|\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\bigl[A\bigl(\zeta_{X+Z-tZ}^{\pm s\pm t(r-s)}\bigr)\mp A(X+Z-tZ)-\frac{1}{2}DA(X+Z-tZ)(s+t(r-s))\bigr]\Bigr|

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigl|\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;DA(X+Z-tZ)(s+t(r-s))-\frac{1}{4}DA(X)(r+s)\Bigr|.

By (
4.84
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\pm}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\,\bigl(|s|^{2}+|r-s|^{2}\bigr),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\,\bigl(|s|+|r-s|\bigr)\,|Z|.

In combination with (
4.89
), these considerations imply (
4.82
). The proof for
\mathbf{A}_{e_{3}}+A
is literally the same.
∎

The next lemma is a substitute for the identity

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(X\wedge Z)}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot P_{X}}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi_{X}}

(4.90)

in the case of a general magnetic field. It holds because
\mathbf{B}\cdot(X\wedge Z)=Z\cdot(\mathbf{B}\wedge X)
and the latter commutes with
Z\cdot\Pi_{X}
. Here, we used the notations
P=-\mathrm{i}\nabla
for the momentum operator and
\Pi=-\mathrm{i}\nabla+2\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}
for its magnetic counterpart. Sometimes when several variables appear in an equation we write, e.g.,
P_{X}
,
\Pi_{X}
, etc. to indicate on which variable
P
,
\Pi
, etc. is acting.

Lemma 4.13
.

Assume that
A\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
is a periodic function. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X+Z)}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi_{X}}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}},

(4.91)

where
\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}=P_{X}+2\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)
is understood to act on the
X
coordinate.

The above lemma is a consequence of the following more abstract proposition, whose proof can be found in
[
37
, p. 290]
. For the sake of completeness we repeat it here.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space, let
P\colon\mathcal{D}(P)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
be a densely defined self-adjoint operator, and assume that
Q
is bounded and self-adjoint. Assume further that
[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tP}\,Q\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tP},\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}sP}\,Q\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}sP}]=0
for every
t,s\in[0,1]
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\exp\Bigl(\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tP}\,Q\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tP}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}P}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(P+Q)}.

Proof.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
we define
Q(s)\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}sP}\,Q\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}sP}
and
W(s)\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s(P+Q)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}sP}
. On
\mathcal{D}(P)
, we may differentiate
W
to get

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}W^{\prime}(s)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s(P+Q)}(P+Q)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}sP}-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s(P+Q)}\,P\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}sP}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s(P+Q)}\,Q\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}sP}=W(s)\,Q(s).

Using that
Q
is bounded we conclude that this identity also holds on
\mathcal{H}
. Hence,
W
satisfies the linear differential equation
W^{\prime}(s)=\mathrm{i}W(s)\,Q(s)
. Since by assumption
[Q(s),Q(t)]=0
holds for all
s,t\in[0,1]
, we conclude that the unique solution to this equation can be written as

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}(s)\coloneqq\exp\Bigl(\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{d}t\;Q(t)\Bigr),

and hence

\displaystyle\exp\Bigl(\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{d}t\;Q(t)\Bigr)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s(P+Q)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}sP}.

With the choice
s=1
this equation proves the claim.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.13
.

We first show that

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X+Z)}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot P_{X}}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(P_{X}+2A_{h}(X))}

(4.92)

holds. To that end, we apply Proposition
4.14
with the choices
P=Z\cdot P_{X}
, where
P_{X}=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X}
, and
Q=2Z\cdot A_{h}(X)
and find

\displaystyle\exp\Bigl(\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tZ\cdot P_{X}}\,2Z\cdot A_{h}(X)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tZ\cdot P_{X}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot P_{X}}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(Z\cdot(P_{X}+2A(X)))}.

It remains to compute the integral in the exponential, which reads

\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tZ\cdot P_{X}}\,2Z\cdot A_{h}(X)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tZ\cdot P_{X}}=2\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\;Z\cdot A_{h}(X+tZ)=\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X+Z).

To obtain the last equality we applied the coordinate transformation
t\mapsto 1-t
. In combination, these considerations prove (
4.92
).

Next, we use (
4.92
) and (
4.90
) to see that

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X+Z)}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi_{X}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X+Z)}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot P_{X}}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(X\wedge Z)}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot(P_{X}+2A_{h}(X))}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{B}\cdot(X\wedge Z)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}}

holds. This proves Lemma
4.13
.
∎

For
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we define the functions

\displaystyle F_{T}^{a}\coloneqq\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{m=0}^{a}\sum_{b=0}^{m}\binom{m}{b}\;\bigl(|\cdot|^{b}\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*\bigl(|\cdot|^{m-b}\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)

(4.93)

and

\displaystyle G_{T}^{a}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\smash{\frac{2}{\beta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{m=0}^{a}\sum_{b=0}^{m}\binom{m}{b}}\;\bigl(|\cdot|^{b}\,|\nabla g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*\bigl(|\cdot|^{m-b}\,|g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt+\bigl(|\cdot|^{b}\,|g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr)*\bigl(|\cdot|^{m-b}\,|\nabla g_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{n}}|\bigr),

(4.94)

which play an prominent role in the proof of Proposition
4.11
. An application of Lemma
4.4
, (
4.56
), and (
4.57
) shows that for
T\geqslant T_{0}>0
and
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\|F_{T}^{a}\|_{1}+\|G_{T}^{a}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{a}.

(4.95)

We are now prepared to give the proof of Proposition
4.11
.

Proof of Proposition
4.11
.

We use (
4.90
) and
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}}(x,y)=\frac{\mathbb{B}}{2}\cdot(x\wedge y)
to write the operator
\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{4}\cdot(r\wedge s)}k_{T}(Z,r-s)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A_{h}}(X,Z,r,s)}\,(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi}\alpha)(X,s),

where
\Pi=-\mathrm{i}\nabla+2\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}
. With the identities

\displaystyle-\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}(r+s)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{B}}{4}\cdot(r\wedge s),

\displaystyle\Phi_{2\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}}(X,X+Z)

\displaystyle=Z\cdot(\mathbf{B}\wedge X),

and (
4.47
) we check that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}}(X,Z,r,s)

\displaystyle=-\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}(X)(r+s)+\Phi_{2\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}}(X,X+Z).

In combination with Lemma
4.13
and the fact that the integrand in the definition of
\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
is an even function of
Z
, this allows us to write
\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle=\smash{\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{4}\cdot(r\wedge s)}k_{T}(Z,r-s)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X+Z)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA_{h}(X)(r+s)}

\displaystyle\hskip 230.0pt\times(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi}\alpha)(X,s),

and consequently,

\displaystyle\bigl(\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\bigr)(X,r)

\displaystyle=-2\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{4}\cdot(r\wedge s)}k_{T}(Z,r-s)\,V\alpha_{*}(s)\,(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi}\Psi)(X)

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt\times\bigl[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A_{h}}(X,Z,r,s)}-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X+Z)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA_{h}(X)(r+s)}\bigr].

(4.96)

We claim that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\;\|D^{2}A_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2}\;\|F_{T}^{3}*|V\alpha_{*}|+F_{T}^{1}*|\cdot|^{2}\,|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;h^{8}\;\max_{k=0,1,2}\|\,|\cdot|^{k}V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}.

(4.97)

The second bound in the above equation follows from Young’s inequality, (
2.7
), and (
4.95
). To prove the first bound in (
4.97
), we use (
4.61
) and find

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\,\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\,\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;|k_{T}(Z,r-s)|\,|V\alpha_{*}(s)|

\displaystyle\hskip-30.0pt\times\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A_{h}}(X,Z,r,s)-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X+Z)+\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA_{h}(X)(r+s)}-1\bigr|\Bigr|^{2}.

(4.98)

Furthermore, an application of Lemma
4.12
implies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;|Z|^{a}\;|k_{T}(Z,r-s)|\,\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A}(X,X+Z)+\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA(X)(r+s)}-1\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip-280.0pt\leqslant C\;\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\bigl[F_{T}^{3+a}(r-s)+F_{T}^{1+a}(r-s)\;|s|^{2}\bigr]

(4.99)

with
F_{T}^{a}
in (
4.93
). We need this bound here only for the case
a=0
but state it for general
a\geqslant 0
for later reference. In combination with (
4.98
), this proves (
4.97
).

We claim that the term involving
\Pi=-\mathrm{i}\nabla+2\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}
, which is understood to act on the center-of-mass coordinate, is bounded by

\displaystyle\|\Pi(\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,h^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}\,\|D^{2}A_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\bigl(1+\|DA_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\times\bigl[\|F_{T}^{4}*|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}+\|F_{T}^{1}*|\cdot|\,|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}+\|F_{T}^{2}*|\cdot|^{2}|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}\bigr].

(4.100)

If this holds, the desired bound for this term follows from Young’s inequality and (
4.95
). To prove (
4.100
), we use (
4.96
) and argue as in the proof of (
4.69
) to see that

\displaystyle\|\Pi(\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant C\,h^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\,\Big(\;\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;\,|V\alpha_{*}(s)|

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt\times|k_{T}(Z,r-s)|\Bigl[h\,(1+|Z|)\,\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A_{h}}(X,Z,r,s)-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X+Z)+\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA_{h}(X)(r+s)}-1\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 45.52458pt+\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\nabla_{X}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A_{h}}(X,Z,r,s)}-\nabla_{X}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X+Z)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA_{h}(X)(r+s)}\bigr|\Bigr]\Bigr|^{2}\Big).

(4.101)

The difference of the phases involving a gradient can be estimated as

\displaystyle\bigl|\nabla_{X}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)}-\nabla_{X}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A}(X,X+Z)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA(X)(r+s)}\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leqslant\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A}(X,X+Z)+\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA(X)(r+s)}-1\bigr||\nabla_{X}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)|

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\bigl|\nabla_{X}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)-\nabla_{X}\Phi_{2A}(X,X+Z)\bigr|+\bigl|\nabla_{X}(r-s)\cdot DA(X)(r+s)\bigr|,

which, by (
4.70
) and Lemma
4.12
, is bounded by

\displaystyle C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\,\bigl(1+\|DA\|_{\infty}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt\times\bigl[\bigl(|s|^{2}+|r-s|^{2}\bigr)\bigl(1+|Z|^{2}+|r-s|^{2}\bigr)+\bigl(|s|+|r-s|\bigr)\bigl(|Z|+|r-s|\bigr)\bigr].

Accordingly,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;|k_{T}(Z,r-s)|\,\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\nabla_{X}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)}-\nabla_{X}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A}(X,X+Z)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA(X)(r+s)}\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt\leqslant C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\bigl(1+\|DA\|_{\infty}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 60.0pt\times\bigl[F_{T}^{4}(r-s)+F_{T}^{1}(r-s)\,|s|+F_{T}^{2}(r-s)\,|s|^{2}\bigr].

Using (
4.99
), we also find

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;|k_{T}(Z,r-s)|\,h\,(1+|Z|)\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A}(X,X+Z)+\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA(X)(r+s)}-1\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt\leqslant C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\;\bigl[F_{T}^{4}(r-s)+F_{T}^{2}(r-s)\,|s|^{2}\bigr].

In combination with (
4.101
), this proves (
4.100
).

We claim that the term involving
\widetilde{\pi}=-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}
, which is understood to act on the relative coordinate, is bounded by

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\pi}(\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,h^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}\,\|D^{2}A_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\bigl(1+\|A_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2}+\|DA_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\bigl\|(F_{T}^{4}+G_{T}^{2})*(1+|\cdot|^{2})|V\alpha_{*}|\bigr\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;h^{8}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}\;\|(1+|\cdot|^{2})V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}.

(4.102)

The second inequality is a consequence of Young’s inequality and (
4.95
). To prove the first inequality in (
4.102
), we first use (
4.96
) and argue as in the proof of (
4.69
) to see that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\pi}(\widetilde{L}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant C\,h^{2}\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\,\Big(\;\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;|V\alpha_{*}(s)|

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\bigl|\widetilde{\pi}k_{T}(Z,r-s)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{4}\cdot(r\wedge s)}\bigr|\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A_{h}}(X,Z,r,s)-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X+Z)+\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA_{h}(X)(r+s)}-1\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+|k_{T}(Z,r-s)|\,\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\nabla_{r}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A_{h}}(X,Z,r,s)}-\nabla_{r}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X+Z)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA_{h}(X)(r+s)}\bigr|\Bigr|^{2}\Big).

A brief computation shows that the operator
\widetilde{\pi}
obeys the following intertwining relation with respect to
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{4}\cdot(r\wedge s)}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{r}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}\cdot(r\wedge s)}=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\mathbf{B}\cdot(r\wedge s)}\bigl(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}+\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{B}\wedge(r-s)\bigr).

(4.103)

The notation
\widetilde{\pi}_{r}
in the above equation highlights on which of the two variables the operator
\widetilde{\pi}
is acting. An application of this identity shows

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\pi}k_{T}(Z,r-s)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{4}(r\wedge s)}|

\displaystyle\leqslant|\nabla_{r}k_{T}(Z,r-s)|+\frac{|\mathbf{B}|}{4}\,|r-s|\,|k_{T}(Z,r-s)|.

(4.104)

Hence, a computation similar to that leading to (
4.99
) shows that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;|\widetilde{\pi}k_{T}(Z,r-s)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{4}\cdot(r\wedge s)}|

\displaystyle\hskip-110.0pt\times\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A}(X,X+Z)+\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA(X)(r+s)}-1\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip-90.0pt\leqslant C\;\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\;\bigl[(F_{T}^{4}+G_{T}^{3})(r-s)+(F_{T}^{2}+G_{T}^{1})(r-s)\;|s|^{2}\bigr]

(4.105)

with the function
F_{T}^{a}
in (
4.93
) and
G_{T}^{a}
in (
4.94
). Let us also note that we estimate the factor
|\mathbf{B}|
coming from the second term in (
4.104
) by
1
.

We also have

\displaystyle\bigl|\nabla_{r}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)}-\nabla_{r}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A}(X,X+Z)-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA(X)(r+s)}\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leqslant\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A}(X,X+Z)+\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA(X)(r+s)}-1\bigr||\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)|

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+\bigl|\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)+\nabla_{r}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA(X)(r+s)\bigr|.

(4.106)

We use

\displaystyle|\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;\bigl(\|A\|_{\infty}+\|DA\|_{\infty}\bigr)\,\bigl(\bigl|Z+\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr|+\bigl|Z-\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr|\bigr)

(4.107)

and Lemma
4.12
to see that the right side of (
4.106
) is bounded by

\displaystyle C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\bigl(1+\|A\|_{\infty}+\|DA\|_{\infty}\bigr)\;\bigl[|s|^{2}+|r-s|^{2}+|Z|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt+\bigl(|s|+|r-s|\bigr)\bigl(|Z|+|r-s|\bigr)\bigl(\bigl|Z+\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr|+\bigl|Z-\frac{r-s}{2}\bigr|\bigr)\bigr].

In combination with (
4.74
) these considerations imply

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;|k_{T}(Z,r-s)|\,\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\nabla_{r}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{A}(X,Z,r,s)}-\nabla_{r}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A}(X,X+Z)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot DA(X)(r+s)}\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip-350.0pt\leqslant C\,\|D^{2}A\|_{\infty}\bigl(1+\|A\|_{\infty}+\|DA\|_{\infty}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip-300.0pt\times\bigl[F_{T}^{3}(r-s)+F_{T}^{2}(r-s)\,|s|+F_{T}^{0}(r-s)\,|s|^{2}\bigr].

When we combine this with (
4.105
), we obtain (
4.102
).
∎

4.5.3.3
The operator
M_{T,\mathbf{A}}
.

We define the operator
M_{T,\mathbf{A}}
by

\displaystyle M_{T,\mathbf{A}}\alpha(X,r)\coloneqq\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\;(\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}})\alpha)(X,s),

(4.108)

where
k_{T}
is defined below (
4.76
). In our calculation, we may replace
\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
by
M_{T,\mathbf{A}}
due to the following error bound.

Proposition 4.15
.

Assume that
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{e_{3}}+A
with a
A\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
periodic, let
|\cdot|^{k}V\alpha_{*}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for
k\in\{0,1,2\}
,
\Psi\in H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})
, and denote
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
). For any
T_{0}>0
there is
h_{0}>0
such that for any
0<h\leqslant h_{0}
and any
T\geqslant T_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-M_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\|_{H^{1}(Q_{h}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;h^{6}\;\bigl(\max_{k\in\{0,1,2\}}\|\,|\cdot|^{k}V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}.

(4.109)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-M_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\;h^{6}\;\bigl(\|V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\,|\cdot|^{2}V\alpha_{*}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}.

(4.110)

Remark 4.16
.

The two bounds in (
4.109
) and (
4.110
) are needed for the proof of Proposition
3.2
and Theorem
3.6
, respectively. We highlight that the bound in (
4.109
) is not strong enough to be useful in the proof of Theorem
3.6
. More precisely, if we apply Cauchy–Schwarz and use Lemma
4.1
as well as (
4.109
) to estimate
\|\Delta\|_{2}
, we obtain a bound that is only of the order
h^{4}
. This is not good enough because
h^{4}
is the order of the Ginzburg–Landau energy. To obtain (
4.110
), we exploit the fact that
V\alpha_{*}
is real-valued, which allows us to replace
\exp(-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)(r+s))
in the definition of
\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}
by
\cos(\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)(r+s))
and to win an additional factor
h^{2}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.15
.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
4.9
. We begin by proving (
4.109
) and claim that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-M_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\,\|D\mathbf{A}_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\|F_{T}^{2}*|V\alpha_{*}|+F_{T}^{1}*|\cdot|\,|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}

(4.111)

with the function
F_{T}^{a}
in (
4.93
). If this holds, the desired bound for this term follows from Young’s inequality, (
2.7
), and the
L^{1}
-norm estimate on
F_{T}^{a}
in (
4.95
). To prove (
4.111
), we argue as in (
4.60
)–(
4.62
) and obtain

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-M_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\,\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-90.0pt\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\,\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;|k_{T}(Z,r-s)|\,\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)(r+s)}-1\bigr|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s)|\Bigr|^{2}.

(4.112)

When we combine the bound

\displaystyle\bigl|(r-s)\cdot D\mathbf{A}(X)(r+s)\bigr|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|D\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\,|r-s|\,\bigl(2|s|+|r-s|\bigr)

(4.113)

and the estimate for
|r-s|
in (
4.74
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;|k_{T}(Z,r-s)|\,\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot D\mathbf{A}(X)(r+s)}-1\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt\leqslant C\,\|D\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\bigl[F_{T}^{2}(r-s)+F_{T}^{1}(r-s)\;|s|\bigr].

(4.114)

In combination with (
4.112
), this proves (
4.111
).

We also claim that the term involving
\Pi
is bounded by

\displaystyle\|\Pi(\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-M_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,h^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-70.0pt\times\bigl[(|\mathbf{B}|^{2}+\|D^{2}A_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2})(1+|\mathbf{B}|^{2}+\|D^{2}A_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2})\,\|F_{T}^{3}*|V\alpha_{*}|+F_{T}^{2}*|\cdot|\,|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-50.0pt+\|D^{2}\mathbf{A}_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\|F_{T}^{2}*|V\alpha_{*}|+F_{T}^{1}*|\cdot|\,|V\alpha_{*}|\,\|_{2}^{2}\bigr].

(4.115)

If this is correct, an application Young’s inequality and (
4.95
) shows the desired bound for this term. To prove (
4.115
), we first
show that

\displaystyle\|\Pi\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}})\Psi\|_{2}\leqslant C\,h^{2}\,(1+|Z|)\,\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}

(4.116)

holds. From Lemma
4.13
we know that

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}}=\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X\mp Z)}

holds. An application of the intertwining relation in (
4.67
) for
\Pi_{X}
and
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi_{X}}
therefore shows

\displaystyle[\Pi,\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}}]=\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}}\bigl[\mp 2\,\mathbf{B}\wedge Z-\nabla_{X}\Phi_{2A_{h}}(X,X\mp Z)\bigr].

We highlight that
\Pi
and
\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}
in the two equations above act on the coordinate
X
. Using Lemma
4.5
, we check that

\displaystyle\nabla_{X}\Phi_{2A}(X,X\mp Z)=2A(X\mp Z)-2A(X)+2\widetilde{A}(X,X\mp Z)-2\widetilde{A}(X\mp Z,X)

holds. Accordingly,
|\nabla_{X}\Phi_{2A}(X,X\mp Z)|\leqslant C\,\|DA\|_{\infty}|Z|
, which implies

\displaystyle\|[\Pi,\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}})]\Psi\|_{2}\leqslant C\,(|\mathbf{B}|+\|DA_{h}\|_{\infty})\,|Z|\,\|\Psi\|_{2}

(4.117)

as well as (
4.116
). Finally, a computation similar to the one leading to (
4.69
), Lemma
4.12
, (
4.114
), (
4.116
), and the above considerations prove (
4.115
). It remains to consider the term proportional to
\widetilde{\pi}
.

With an argument that is similar to the one leading to (
4.72
), we see that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\pi}(\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-M_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\,\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-100.0pt\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\,\Bigl|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;\bigl|\widetilde{\pi}k_{T}(Z,r-s)\bigl[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)\cdot(r+s)}-1\bigr]\bigr|\;|V\alpha_{*}(s)|\Bigr|^{2}.

We also note that

\displaystyle\bigl|\nabla_{r}(r-s)D\mathbf{A}(X)(r+s)\bigr|\leqslant C\,\|D\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\bigl(|s|+|r-s|\bigr).

In combination with (
4.113
) and
|\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}(r)|\leqslant|\mathbf{B}|\ (|r-s|+|s|)
, this implies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;\bigl|\widetilde{\pi}k_{T}(Z,r-s)\bigl[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot D\mathbf{A}(X)\cdot(r+s)}-1\bigr]\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leqslant C\,(|\mathbf{B}|+\|DA\|_{\infty})\;\bigl((F_{T}^{2}+G_{T}^{2})(r-s)+(F_{T}^{0}+G_{T}^{1})(r-s)\;|s|\bigr)

with the function
F_{T}^{a}
in (
4.93
) and
G_{T}^{a}
in (
4.94
). When we apply Young’s inequality and (
4.95
), we obtain (
4.109
). It remains to prove (
4.110
).

To that end, we need to consider

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-M_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\rangle

\displaystyle=4\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\;\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)\cdot(r+s)}-1\bigr)V\alpha_{*}(r)V\alpha_{*}(s)

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\fint_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}})\Psi(X).

(4.118)

The left side of this equation is real-valued. It therefore equals
1/2
times the right side plus
1/2
times the complex conjugate of the right side. When we use that
V\alpha_{*}
is real-valued, that the Matsubara frequencies in (
3.10
) satisfy
-\omega_{n}=\omega_{-(n+1)}
, and the transformation
n\mapsto-n-1
in the sum in the definition of
k_{T}(Z,r-s)
, we see that the complex conjugate of the right side equals the same expression with
\exp(-\mathrm{i}\frac{r-s}{4}\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)(r+s))
replaced by its complex conjugate. Using this and the identity
\cos(x)-1=-2\sin^{2}(\frac{x}{2})
we find

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-M_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\rangle

\displaystyle=-8\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\;\sin^{2}\left(\frac{(r-s)}{8}\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)\cdot(r+s)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt\times V\alpha_{*}(r)V\alpha_{*}(s)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\fint_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\;\overline{\Psi(X)}\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}})\Psi(X).

(4.119)

Furthermore, (
4.113
) implies

\displaystyle\sin^{2}\bigl(\frac{1}{8}(r-s)\cdot D\mathbf{A}_{h}(X)(r+s)\bigr)\leqslant C\,\|D\mathbf{A}_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2}\,|r-s|^{2}\,\bigl(|s|^{2}+|r-s|^{2}\bigr).

We use this bound, (
4.119
), and
\|\cos(Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}})\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1
to see that

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta,\widetilde{M}_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta-M_{T,\mathbf{A}}\Delta\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|D\mathbf{A}_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2}\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}\;\bigl\||V\alpha_{*}|\;\bigl(F_{T}^{4}*|V\alpha_{*}|+F_{T}^{2}*|\cdot|^{2}|V\alpha_{*}|\bigr)\bigr\|_{1}.

The desired bound in (
4.110
) follows when we apply Young’s inequality and use (
2.7
) as well as the
L^{1}
-norm estimate for
F_{T}^{a}
in (
4.95
). This completes the proof of Proposition
4.15
.
∎

4.5.4
Analysis of
M_{T,\mathbf{A}}
and calculation of two quadratic terms in the Ginzburg–Landau functional

We decompose as
M_{T,\mathbf{A}}=M_{T}^{(1)}+M_{T,\mathbf{A}}^{(2)}+M_{T,\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}
, where

\displaystyle M_{T}^{(1)}\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\;\alpha(X,s),

(4.120)

\displaystyle M_{T,\mathbf{A}}^{(2)}\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\;(Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}})^{2}\;\alpha(X,s),

(4.121)

\displaystyle M_{T,\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}\alpha(X,r)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}s\;k_{T}(Z,r-s)\;\mathcal{R}(Z\cdot\Pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}})\;\alpha(X,s),

(4.122)

and
\mathcal{R}(x)=\cos(x)-1+\frac{1}{2}x^{2}
.

4.5.4.1
The operator
M_{T}^{(1)}
.

From the quadratic form
\langle\Delta,M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta\rangle
we extract the quadratic term without external fields or a gradient in the Ginzburg–Landau functional in (
1.18
). We also obtain a term that cancels the last term on the left side of (
3.6
). The relevant computation can be found in
[
16
, Proposition 4.11]
. For the sake of completeness, we state the result also here. We recall that
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}=-2V\alpha_{*}\Psi
as in (
3.1
).

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
V\alpha_{*}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\Psi\in L_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})
, and
\Delta\equiv\Delta_{\Psi}
as in (
3.1
). Then the following statements hold.

(a)

We have
M_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}^{(1)}\Delta(X,r)=-2\,\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi(X)
.

(b)

For any
T_{0}>0
there is a constant
c>0
such that for
T_{0}\leqslant T\leqslant{T_{\mathrm{c}}}
we have

\displaystyle\langle\Delta,M_{T}^{(1)}\Delta-M_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}^{(1)}\Delta\rangle\geqslant c\,\frac{{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-T}{{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}\;\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}.

(c)

Given
D\in\mathbb{R}
there is
h_{0}>0
such that for
0<h\leqslant h_{0}
, and
T={T_{\mathrm{c}}}(1-Dh^{2})
we have
