) appears as a lower bound for the full BCS functional. Despite of its apparent simplicity, it still contains all the information needed for a proof of Theorem
5.1
. Before we state the corollary, let us define the operator

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r}{2}\Pi_{X}},

(5.15)

with
\Pi_{X}
in (
2.4
), which acts on the relative coordinate
r=x-y
as well as on the center-of-mass coordinate
X=\frac{x+y}{2}
of a function
\alpha(X,r)
.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let Assumptions
1.1
and
1.2
be true. For any
D_{0},D_{1}\geqslant 0
, there is a constant
h_{0}>0
such that if
\Gamma
is a low-energy state in the sense that it satisfies (
5.1
), if
0<h\leqslant h_{0}
, and if
T
is such that
T-{T_{\mathrm{c}}}\geqslant-D_{0}h^{2}
, then
\alpha=\Gamma_{12}
obeys

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,[U(1-P)(1+\pi^{2})(1-P)U^{*}+U^{*}(1-P)(1+\pi^{2})(1-P)U]\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 200.0pt+\Tr\bigl[(\alpha^{*}\alpha)^{2}\bigr]\leqslant Ch^{2}\|\alpha\|_{2}^{2}+D_{1}h^{4},

(5.16)

where
P=|\alpha_{*}\rangle\langle\alpha_{*}|
and
\pi=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}(r)
both act on the relative coordinate
r
of
\alpha(X,r)
.

In the statement of the corollary and in the following, we refrain from equipping the operator
\pi
and the projection
P=|\alpha_{*}\rangle\langle\alpha_{*}|
with an index
r
although it acts on the relative coordinate. This does not lead to confusion and keeps the formulas readable. The proof of the corollary is inspired by the proof of
[
19
, Proposition 23]
.

Proof of Corollary
5.6
.

In the following we use the notation
\pi_{\mathbf{A}}^{x}=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{x}+\mathbf{A}(x)
. We claim that

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{x}

\displaystyle=U\pi_{\mathbf{A}^{+}_{h}}^{r}U^{*},

\displaystyle-\pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{y}

\displaystyle=U^{*}\pi_{\mathbf{A}^{-}_{h}}^{r}U,

(5.17)

where
\pi_{\mathbf{A}^{\pm}}^{r}=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}+\mathbf{A}^{\pm}(r)
with

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{\pm}(r)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{A}_{e_{3}}(r)\pm A(X\pm r).

To obtain (
5.17
), we denote
P_{X}=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{X}
and note that
[r\cdot P_{X},r\cdot(\mathbf{B}\wedge X)]=0
implies
U=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathbf{B}}{2}(X\wedge r)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{r}{2}P_{X}}
. Using this identity we conclude that

\displaystyle U\;(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}(r))\,U^{*}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}(r)-\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{X},

\displaystyle U^{*}(-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}(r))\,U\;

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}(r)+\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{X},

where
\Pi=-\mathrm{i}\nabla+2\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}
. Eq. (
5.17
) is a direct consequence of these two identities.

We also have

\displaystyle W(x)

\displaystyle=U\,W^{+}(r)\,U^{*},

\displaystyle W(y)

\displaystyle=U^{*}\,W^{-}(r)\,U,

\displaystyle W^{\pm}(r)

\displaystyle\coloneqq W(X\pm r).

Consequently, if
K_{T,\mathbf{A},W}^{x}
and
K_{T,\mathbf{A},W}^{y}
denote the operators
K_{T,\mathbf{A},W}
acting on the
x
and
y
coordinate, respectively, we infer

\displaystyle K_{T,\mathbf{A},W}^{x}-V(r)

\displaystyle=U^{*}(K_{T,\mathbf{A}^{+},W^{+}}^{r}-V(r))\,U,

\displaystyle K_{T,\mathbf{A},W}^{y}-V(r)

\displaystyle=U\;(K_{T,\mathbf{A}^{-},W^{-}}^{r}-V(r))\,U^{*}.

(5.18)

We highlight that
\mathbf{A}^{\pm}
and
W^{\pm}
depend on
X
.

The operator
V
in (
5.12
) acts by multiplication with the function
V(x-y)
. We use the symmetry
\alpha(x,y)=\alpha(y,x)
to deduce

\displaystyle\Tr\bigl[(K_{T,\mathbf{A},W}-V)\alpha\alpha^{*}\bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip-80.0pt=\frac{1}{2}\fint_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}y\;\overline{\alpha(x,y)}\bigl[(K_{T,\mathbf{A},W}^{x}-V)+(K_{T,\mathbf{A},W}^{y}-V)\bigr]\alpha(x,y).

(5.19)

In combination, (
5.1
), (
5.12
), (
5.18
), and (
5.19
) therefore prove the bound

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\alpha,[U^{*}(K_{T,\mathbf{A}^{+},W^{+}}^{r}-V(r))\,U+U\;(K_{T,\mathbf{A}^{-},W^{-}}^{r}-V(r))\,U^{*}]\alpha\rangle+c\Tr\bigl[(\alpha^{*}\alpha)^{2}\bigr]\leqslant D_{1}h^{4}.

An application of Lemma
5.4
on the left side establishes (
5.16
).
∎

5.2
Proof of Theorem
5.1

The statement in Theorem
5.1
is a direct consequence of Corollary
5.6
above and the results in
[
16
]
. More precisely, we need to combine Corollary
5.6
,
[
16
, Proposition 5.7]
,
[
16
, Lemma 5.14]
, and the arguments in
[
16
, Section 5.4]
.

6
The Lower Bound on (
1.22
) and Proof of Theorem
2
(b)

6.1
The BCS energy of low-energy states

In this section, we complete the proofs of Theorems
1
and
2
, which amounts to providing the lower bound on (
1.22
), the bound in (
1.27
), and the proof of Theorem
2
(b). Since these proofs mostly go along the same lines as those in
[
16
]
, we only mention the differences and keep the presentation to a minimal length. Once the a priori estimates in
[
16
, Theorem 5.1]
are proved, the proofs of the lower bound for the free energy, the decomposition of the Cooper pair wave function of an approximate minimizer, and the upper bound for the critical temperature shift in
[
16
]
follow the same strategy as the related proofs in
[
21
,
22
]
. In the following we will, however, only refer to
[
16
]
because our presentation is closer to the analysis in this reference than to those in
[
21
,
22
]
.

Let
D_{1}\geqslant 0
and
D\in\mathbb{R}
be given, choose
T={T_{\mathrm{c}}}(1-Dh^{2})
, and assume that
\Gamma
is a gauge-periodic state that satisfies (
5.1
). Corollary
5.2
guarantees a decomposition of the Cooper pair wave function
\alpha=[\Gamma]_{12}
in terms of
\Psi_{\leqslant}
in (
5.4
) and
\sigma
in (
5.9
). The function
\Psi_{\leqslant}
satisfies the bounds

\displaystyle\|\Psi_{\leqslant}\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}\leqslant C,

\displaystyle\|\Psi_{\leqslant}\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{2}(Q_{h})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\varepsilon h^{-2}\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2},

(6.1)

with
\Psi
in (
5.2
). Let us define the state
\Gamma_{\Delta}
as in (
3.4
) with
\Delta(X,r)=-2V\alpha_{*}(r)\Psi_{\leqslant}(X)
. We apply Proposition
3.5
and Theorem
3.6
to obtain the following lower bound for the BCS energy of
\Gamma
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle\geqslant h^{4}\;\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D}(\Psi_{\leqslant})-C\left(h^{5}+\varepsilon h^{4}\right)\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt+\frac{T}{2}\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Gamma,\Gamma_{\Delta})-\fint_{Q_{h}}\mathrm{d}X\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}r\;V(r)\,|\sigma(X,r)|^{2}.

(6.2)

In the next section we prove a lower bound for the terms in the second line of (
6.2
).

6.2
Estimate on the relative entropy

The arguments in
[
16
, Eqs. (6.1)-(6.14)]
apply in literally the same way here, too. We obtain the correct bounds when we replace
B
by
h^{2}
in all formulas. This, in particular, applies to the statement of
[
16
, Lemma 6.2]
. The only difference is that
[
16
, Eq. (6.10)]
is now given by

|\langle\eta_{0},K_{T_{\mathrm{c}},\mathbf{A},W}\sigma\rangle|\leqslant\ C\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}h^{\nicefrac{{9}}{{2}}}\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}\bigl(\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}+D_{1}\bigr)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},

which is due to the reason that the bound for the
L^{2}
-norm of
\eta_{0}
in Proposition
3.2
is worse than the comparable bound we obtained in
[
16
, Proposition 3.2]
. This, however, does not change the size of the remainder in the final bound because other error terms come with a worse rate.

With the choice
\varepsilon=h^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}
, we therefore obtain the bound

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma)-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{BCS}}_{h,T}(\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\geqslant h^{4}\,\bigl(\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{GL}}_{D}(\Psi_{\leqslant})-C\,h^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{6}}}\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}\;\bigl(\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}^{2}+D_{1}\bigr)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\bigr),

(6.3)

which is the equivalent of
[
16
, Eq (6.14)]
.

6.3
Conclusion

The arguments in
[
16
, Section 6.3]
apply in the same way also here and we obtain the correct formulas when we replace
B^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
by
h
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
.

6.4
Proof of the equivalent of
[
16
, Lemma 6.2]
in our setting

To obtain a proof of the equivalent of
[
16
, Lemma 6.2]
in our setting, we follow the proof strategy in
[
16
]
. The additional terms coming from the external electric potential are not difficult to bound because
W
is a bounded function. To obtain bounds of the correct size in
h
for the terms involving the periodic vector potential
A_{h}
, we need to use that
A(0)=0
, which is guaranteed by Assumption
1.1
. This is relevant for example when we estimate our equivalent of the term on the left side of
[
16
, Eq. (6.24)]
, that is,

\|[\pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{2}+W_{h}(r)-p_{r}^{2}]\sigma_{0}\|_{2}

with
p_{r}=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}
and
\sigma_{0}
in (
5.7
). We write the operator multiplying
\sigma_{0}
as

\pi_{r}^{2}-p_{r}^{2}+W(r)+A(r)\cdot\pi_{r}+\pi_{r}\cdot A(r)+|A(r)|^{2},

(6.4)

where
\pi_{r}=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{r}+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}}(r)
. When we use (
5.6
), we see that the terms involving
|A_{h}|^{2}
and
W_{h}
are bounded by

\displaystyle\bigl(\|A_{h}\|_{\infty}^{2}+\|W_{h}\|_{\infty}\bigr)\ \|\sigma_{0}\|_{2}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}h^{4}\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}.

(6.5)

Moreover, from
[
16
, Eq. (6.24)]
we know that

\|[\pi_{r}^{2}-p_{r}^{2}]\sigma_{0}\|_{2}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}h^{4}\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}.

To obtain a bound for the contribution from the fourth and the fifth term on the right side of (
6.4
), we write

A_{h}(r)=h^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\ (DA)(hrt)\cdot r,

where
DA
denotes the Jacobi matrix of
A
. Hence,

\|A_{h}(r)\cdot\pi_{r}\,\sigma_{0}\|_{2}\leqslant\ h^{2}\|DA\|_{\infty}\,\|\ |\cdot|\pi\alpha_{*}\|_{2}\,\|\Psi_{>}\|_{2}\leqslant Ch^{4}\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}.

The term involving
\pi_{r}\cdot A_{h}(r)
can be treated similarly when we commute
\pi_{r}
to the right. In combination, the above considerations show

\|[\pi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{2}+W_{h}(r)-p_{r}^{2}]\sigma_{0}\|_{2}\leqslant Ch^{4}\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\|\Psi\|_{H_{\mathrm{mag}}^{1}(Q_{h})}.

All other bounds in the proof of the equivalent of
[
16
, Lemma 6.2]
in our setting that involve
W_{h}
or
A_{h}
can be estimated with similar ideas. We therefore omit further details.

— Appendix —

Appendix A
Gauge-Invariant Perturbation Theory for
K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}-V

In this appendix, we discuss the behavior of the eigenvalues below the essential spectrum of the operator
K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}-V
for small
h>0
, where
K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}\coloneqq K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A},0}
with
K_{T,\mathbf{A},0}
defined in (
5.13
). We recall that the full magnetic vector potential is given by
\mathbf{A}\coloneqq\mathbf{A}_{e_{3}}+A
with the magnetic vector potential
\mathbf{A}_{e_{3}}(x)=\frac{1}{2}e_{3}\wedge x
of a constant magnetic field and a periodic vector potential
A
that satisfies
A(0)=0
. We also recall the notation
\mathbf{A}_{h}(x)\coloneqq h\mathbf{A}(hx)
. The goal of this appendix is to prove the following proposition.

Proposition A.1
.

Assume
(1+|\cdot|^{2})V\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and let
A\in W^{3,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
be a periodic function. Then there is
h_{0}>0
such that for
0<h\leqslant h_{0}
the following statements hold:

(a)

Let
\lambda
be an isolated eigenvalue of multiplicity
m\in\mathbb{N}
of the operator
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V
with spectral projection
P
. Then there are
m
eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}(h),\ldots,\lambda_{m}(h)
of the operator
K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}-V
with spectral projection
P(h)
such that

\displaystyle\max_{i=1,\ldots,m}|\lambda_{i}(h)-\lambda|

\displaystyle\leqslant Ch^{2},

\displaystyle\|P(h)-P\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant Ch^{2}.

(A.1)

(b)

Assume that
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V
has a simple lowest eigenvalue with eigenfunction
\alpha_{*}
and denote by
\alpha_{*}^{h}
the eigenfunction to the lowest eigenvalue of
K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}-V
, which is normalized such that
\langle\alpha_{*},\alpha_{*}^{h}\rangle\geqslant 0
holds. Then we have the bound

\displaystyle\|(1+\pi^{2})(\alpha_{*}^{h}-\alpha_{*})\|_{2}\leqslant Ch^{2},

(A.2)

where
\pi^{2}=(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{B}})^{2}
denotes the magnetic Laplacian.

Remark A.2
.

The above Proposition should be compared to
[
16
, Proposition A.1]
, where a similar statement is proved in the special case, where
V\geqslant 0
and where
\mathbf{A}(x)=\frac{1}{2}e_{3}\wedge x
. In the reference the assumption for
V
is needed to assure that the Birman–Schwinger operator related to
K_{T,\mathbf{A}}-V
is self-adjoint. To prove the above proposition we investigate the resolvent kernel of
K_{T,\mathbf{A}}-V
rather than the Birman–Schwinger operator, which is the reason why we do not need the positivity assumption for
V
. The main technical ingredient of our proof is the estimate for the integral kernel of the resolvent of
K_{T}-V
in Lemma
A.4
below. Afterwards, we apply a standard version of gauge-invariant perturbation theory. As has been shown in
[
40
, Chapter 6]
one can also obtain exponential decay estimates for the resolvent kernel of
K_{T}-V
via Combes–Thomas estimates. The proof, however, requires more effort than for the case of the Laplacian because
K_{T}
has a more complicated structure. Since these estimates are not needed for the proof of Proposition
A.1
,we refrain from presenting them here.

We denote the resolvent of
K_{T,\mathbf{A}}-V
at
z\in\rho(K_{T,\mathbf{A}}-V)
by
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{A}}^{z,V}\coloneqq(z-(K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}-V))^{-1}
. The integral kernel of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{A}}^{z,V}
is denoted by
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{A}}^{z,V}(x,y)
. We highlight that we use another symbol for the resolvent and for its integral kernel in this section. Since we frequently work with
L^{p}
-norms of integral kernels this simplifies our notation. If
\mathbf{A}=0
, we write
\mathcal{R}^{z,V}
for
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{z,V}
and
\mathcal{G}^{z,V}
for
\mathcal{G}_{0}^{z,V}
. Similarly,
\mathcal{G}^{z}
stands for
\mathcal{G}^{z,0}
. Before we give the proof of the above proposition, we state and prove four preparatory lemmas.

A.1
Preparatory lemmas

The first lemma concerns the regularity of the kernel
\mathcal{G}^{z}
.

Lemma A.3
.

There is a continuous function
a\colon\mathbb{C}\backslash[2{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\infty)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}x\ (1+x^{2})|\nabla\mathcal{G}^{z}(x)|\leqslant a(z).

(A.3)

Proof.

We use the resolvent identity
\mathcal{R}^{z}=\mathcal{R}^{0}+z\mathcal{R}^{0}\mathcal{R}^{z}
to write
\mathcal{G}^{z}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{z}(x)=\mathcal{G}^{0}(x)+z\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}v\;\mathcal{G}^{0}(x-v)\,\mathcal{G}^{z}(v),

(A.4)

which implies

\displaystyle\|(1+|\cdot|^{2})\nabla\mathcal{G}^{z}\|_{1}\leqslant\|(1+2|\cdot|^{2})\nabla\mathcal{G}^{0}\|_{1}\left(1+|z|\,\|(1+|\cdot|^{2})\mathcal{G}^{z}\|_{1}\right).

(A.5)

The second
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-norm on the right side of (
A.5
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\|(1+|\cdot|^{2})\mathcal{G}^{z}\|_{1}\leqslant C\ \|(1+|\cdot|^{2})^{2}\mathcal{G}^{z}\|_{2}=C\,\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}p\ \Bigl|(1-\Delta_{p})^{2}\frac{1}{z-K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)}\Bigr|^{2}\Bigr)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},

(A.6)

which, when multiplied with
z
, meets the requirements of the lemma. It therefore remains to consider
\|(1+|\cdot|^{2})\nabla\mathcal{G}^{0}\|_{1}
.

From
[
21
, Eq. (A.6)]
and
[
39
, Theorem 6.23]
we know that
\mathcal{G}^{0}(x)
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n-\frac{1}{2}}\text{Im}\ g_{0}^{\mathrm{i}(n-\frac{1}{2})2\pi{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}(x)

(A.7)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}|x|}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n-\frac{1}{2}}\text{Im}\exp\Bigl(\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\mu+\mathrm{i}\left(n-\frac{1}{2}\right)2\pi{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}\,|x|\Bigr),

where
g_{0}^{z}
is the resolvent kernel of the Laplacian in (
4.3
) and
\sqrt{\cdot}
denotes the principal square root. We use
\Im\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z}=\sin(\Re z)\exp(-\Im z)
for
z\in\mathbb{C}
and

\displaystyle\sqrt{a+\mathrm{i}b}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}+a}+\frac{\mathrm{i}\sgn(b)}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}-a}

(A.8)

for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
to see that

\displaystyle\text{Im}\exp\Bigl(\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\mu+\mathrm{i}\left(n-\frac{1}{2}\right)2\pi{T_{\mathrm{c}}}}\,|x|\Bigr)=\sin\left(|x|c_{n}^{+}\right)\exp\left(-|x|c_{n}^{-}\right),

(A.9)

where

\displaystyle c_{n}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\sqrt{\mu^{2}+((n-1/2)2\pi{T_{\mathrm{c}}})^{2}}\pm\mu}.

(A.10)

In particular,

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{G}^{0}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{x}{2\pi^{2}|x|^{3}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n-\frac{1}{2}}\sin\left(|x|c_{n}^{+}\right)\exp\left(-|x|c_{n}^{-}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt+\frac{x}{2\pi^{2}|x|^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}^{+}}{n-\frac{1}{2}}\cos\left(|x|c_{n}^{+}\right)\exp\left(-|x|c_{n}^{-}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt-\frac{x}{2\pi^{2}|x|^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}^{-}}{n-\frac{1}{2}}\sin\left(|x|c_{n}^{+}\right)\exp\left(-|x|c_{n}^{-}\right).

(A.11)

The above formula implies the bound

\displaystyle\|(1+|\cdot|^{2})\nabla\mathcal{G}^{0}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{C}{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}r\left(1+r^{2}+(r+r^{3})\sqrt{1+n}\right)\exp\left(-rc_{n}^{-}\right)\leqslant C\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-\frac{3}{2}}.

(A.12)

This proves the claim of the lemma.
∎

The second lemma concerns bounds for the operator norm of
(z-(K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V))^{-1}
and commutators of this operator with
x
, when viewed as maps from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
to
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Here and in the following we denote by
\|A\|_{2;\infty}
the norm of a bounded operator
A
from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\rightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. From
[
47
, Corollary A.1.2]
we know that if such an operator is also a bounded from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
then it has an integral kernel given by a measurable function
A(x,y)
, which obeys

\displaystyle\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}y\;|A(x,y)|^{2}\right)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}<\infty.

(A.13)

Moreover, the norm
\|A\|_{2;\infty}
equals the norm of the integral kernel of
A
in (
A.13
).

The following Lemma
A.4
implies that the resolvent kernel
\mathcal{G}^{z,V}
satisfies

\displaystyle\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}y\;\bigl(1+|x-y|^{4}\bigr)|\mathcal{G}^{z,V}(x,y)|^{2}\Bigr)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}<\infty.

(A.14)

In passing we note that our assumptions on
V
would allow for more: it can be shown that
\mathcal{G}^{z,V}
is exponentially decaying in the sense that (
A.14
) holds with
1+|x-y|^{4}
replaced by
\mathrm{e}^{\delta|x-y|}
for some
\delta>0
depending on the distance of
z
to the spectrum of
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V
. For more information we refer to Remark
A.2
above.

Lemma A.4
.

Assume that
V\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
vanishes at infinity. Then there is a continuous function
a\colon\rho(K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}^{z,V}\|_{2;\infty}+\bigl\|[x,\mathcal{R}^{z,V}]\bigr\|_{2;\infty}+\bigl\|[x,[x,\mathcal{R}^{z,V}]]\bigr\|_{2;\infty}\leqslant a(z).

(A.15)

Proof.

We start by proving the bound for the first term on the right side of (
A.15
). We use the fact that
(1-\Delta)^{-1}
is a bounded linear map from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
to
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and the resolvent identity to estimate

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}^{z,V}\|_{2;\infty}\leqslant C\|(1-\Delta)\mathcal{R}^{z,V}\|_{\infty}\leqslant C\|(1-\Delta)\mathcal{R}^{z}\|_{\infty}\left(1+\|V\|_{\infty}\|\mathcal{R}^{z,V}\|_{\infty}\right).

(A.16)

Our assumptions on
V
imply
\rho(K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V)\subseteq\rho(K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}})
, whence both
z
-dependent terms on the right side meet the requirements of the lemma.

To obtain a bound for the second term on the right side of (
A.15
), we note that

\displaystyle[x,\mathcal{R}^{z,V}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{z,V}[K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}},x]\mathcal{R}^{z,V},

\displaystyle[K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}},x]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}(\nabla f)(-\mathrm{i}\nabla),

(A.17)

where
f(p)\coloneqq K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)
is the symbol in (
1.14
). Using this, we estimate

\displaystyle\|[x,\mathcal{R}^{z,V}]\|_{2;\infty}\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}^{z,V}\|_{2;\infty}\,\|(\nabla f)(-\mathrm{i}\nabla)\mathcal{R}^{z,V}\|_{\infty}.

(A.18)

A bound for the first factor on the right side was obtained in (
A.16
). Using the resolvent identity again, we bound the second factor by

\displaystyle\|(\nabla f)(-\mathrm{i}\nabla)\mathcal{R}^{z,V}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\|(\nabla f)(-\mathrm{i}\nabla)\mathcal{R}^{z}\|_{\infty}\left(1+\|V\|_{\infty}\|\mathcal{R}^{z,V}\|_{\infty}\right),

(A.19)

which proves the claim for the second term on the right side of (
A.15
). A bound for the third term can be derived similarly, and we therefore leave the remaining details to the reader. This proves the claim.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

Assume
(1+|\cdot|^{2})V\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and let
\alpha
be an eigenfunction of the operator
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V
with eigenvalue
\lambda<2{T_{\mathrm{c}}}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\|\,|\cdot|\nabla\alpha\|_{2}+\|\,|\cdot|^{2}\alpha\|_{2}<\infty.

(A.20)

Proof.

We use the eigenvalue equation to write the Fourier transform of
\alpha
as

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}(p)=-\frac{1}{\lambda-K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)}\,(\widehat{V}\ast\widehat{\alpha})(p).

(A.21)

Using Young’s inequality, we see that this implies

\displaystyle\|\,|\cdot|^{2}\alpha\|

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}p\;\Bigl|\Delta_{p}\frac{1}{\lambda-K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)}\,(\widehat{V}\ast\widehat{\alpha})(p)\Bigr|^{2}\Bigr)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\left(\|\widehat{V}\ast\widehat{\alpha}\|_{\infty}+\|\Delta(\widehat{V}\ast\widehat{\alpha})\|_{\infty}\right)\leqslant C\,\|(1+|\cdot|^{2})V\|_{\infty}.

(A.22)

To prove the other bound, we use the resolvent identity to write (
A.21
) as

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}(p)=-\frac{1}{K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)}\,(\widehat{V}\ast\widehat{\alpha})(p)+\frac{\lambda}{K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)}\frac{1}{\lambda-K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)}\,(\widehat{V}\ast\widehat{\alpha})(p).

(A.23)

We argue as in (
A.22
) to see that the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-norm of
\nabla_{p}p\frac{\lambda}{K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)}\frac{1}{\lambda-K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}(p)}(\widehat{V}\ast\widehat{\alpha})(p)
is bounded by a constant times
\|\,|\cdot|V\|_{\infty}
. To treat the other term, we go back to position space and note that

\displaystyle\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}x\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}y\;|x|\,|\nabla\mathcal{G}^{0}(x-y)|\,|V\alpha(y)|\right|^{2}\Bigr)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\leqslant\|(1+|\cdot|)\nabla\mathcal{G}^{0}\|_{1}\,\|(1+|\cdot|)V\|_{\infty}.

(A.24)

In combination with Lemma
A.3
, these considerations prove the claim.
∎

The last lemma provides us with a convenient representation for the operator
K_{T,\mathbf{A}}
. Its proof can be found in
[
16
, Lemma 6.4]
. Before we can state the lemma we need the following definition.

Definition A.6

(Speaker path)
.

Let
R>0
, assume that
\mu\geqslant-1
and define the following complex paths

\displaystyle\begin{split}u_{1}(t)&\coloneqq\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}}+(1+\mathrm{i})t\\
u_{2}(t)&\coloneqq\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}}-(\mu+1)t\\
u_{3}(t)&\coloneqq-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}}t-(\mu+1)\\
u_{4}(t)&\coloneqq-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}}-(\mu+1)(1-t)\\
u_{5}(t)&\coloneqq-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}}+(1-\mathrm{i})t\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phantom{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}}}t&\in[0,R],\\
\phantom{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}}}t&\in[0,1],\\
\phantom{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}}}t&\in[-1,1],\\
\phantom{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}}}t&\in[0,1],\\
\phantom{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{{\beta_{\mathrm{c}}}}}t&\in[0,R].\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{\includegraphics[width]{Speaker_path.pdf}}\end{split}

The speaker path is defined as the union of paths
u_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,5
, with
u_{1}
taken in reverse direction, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\hskip 0.7pt\text{\raisebox{-1.7pt}{\scalebox{1.4}{\faIconFromMacro{faVolumeOff}}}\hskip 0.7pt}}_{R}\coloneqq\mathop{\dot{-}}u_{1}\mathop{\dot{+}}u_{2}\mathop{\dot{+}}u_{3}\mathop{\dot{+}}u_{4}\mathop{\dot{+}}u_{5}.

If
\mu<-1
we choose the same path as in the case
\mu=-1
.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
H\colon\mathcal{D}(H)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
be a self-adjoint operator on a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with
H\geqslant-\mu
and let
\beta>0
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{H}{\tanh(\frac{\beta}{2}H)}=H+\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{{\hskip 0.7pt\text{\raisebox{-1.7pt}{\scalebox{1.4}{\faIconFromMacro{faVolumeOff}}}\hskip 0.7pt}}_{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\Bigl(\frac{z}{\tanh(\frac{\beta}{2}z)}-z\Bigr)\frac{1}{z-H},

(A.25)

with the speaker path
{\hskip 0.7pt\text{\raisebox{-1.7pt}{\scalebox{1.4}{\faIconFromMacro{faVolumeOff}}}\hskip 0.7pt}}_{R}
in Definition
A.6
. The above integral including the limit is understood as an improper Riemann integral with respect to the uniform operator topology.

In the following, we use the symbol
\int_{{\hskip 0.7pt\text{\raisebox{-1.7pt}{\scalebox{1.4}{\faIconFromMacro{faVolumeOff}}}\hskip 0.7pt}}}
to denote the integral on the right side of (
A.25
) including the limit and we denote
{\hskip 0.7pt\text{\raisebox{-1.7pt}{\scalebox{1.4}{\faIconFromMacro{faVolumeOff}}}\hskip 0.7pt}}=\bigcup_{R>0}{\hskip 0.7pt\text{\raisebox{-1.7pt}{\scalebox{1.4}{\faIconFromMacro{faVolumeOff}}}\hskip 0.7pt}}_{R}
.

A.2
Proof of Proposition
A.1

The proof of Proposition
A.1
is based on an adaption of gauge-invariant perturbation theory for Schrödinger operators as introduced in
[
42
]
to our setting. The core of the argument is contained in the following lemma.

Lemma A.8
.

Assume that
(1+|\cdot|^{2})V\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. There is a continuous function
a\colon\rho(K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that the following holds: For every compact set
K\subset\rho(K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V)
there is a constant
h(K)>0
such that for
0<h<h(K)
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{A}}^{z,V}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{z,V}+h^{2}\,\eta_{h}(z)\quad\text{ with }\quad\|(1+\pi^{2})\eta_{h}(z)\|_{\infty}\leqslant a(z).

(A.26)

Here
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}}^{z,V}
denotes the operator defined by the kernel

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}}^{z,V}(x,y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)}\,\mathcal{G}^{z,V}(x,y)

(A.27)

with the phase factor
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)
in (
4.2
).

Proof.

We employ (
4.18
) and apply the integral representation in Lemma
A.7
to
K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}
to see that

\displaystyle K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}^{x}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(x,y)}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(x,y)}\,K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}_{y}}^{x}

(A.28)

holds. Here
K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}^{x}
is understood to act on the
x
-coordinate and
\mathbf{A}_{y}(x)\coloneqq\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)
denotes the vector potential in transversal Poincaré gauge relative to the point
y
, defined in (
4.12
). Using Lemma
A.7
again, we also find the identity

\displaystyle K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}_{y}}-K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}

\displaystyle=\left(-2\mathrm{i}(\mathbf{A}_{h})_{y}(x)\nabla_{x}-\mathrm{i}\divv(\mathbf{A}_{h})_{y}(x)+|(\mathbf{A}_{h})_{y}(x)|^{2}\right)

(A.29)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt+\int_{{\hskip 0.7pt\text{\raisebox{-1.7pt}{\scalebox{1.4}{\faIconFromMacro{faVolumeOff}}}\hskip 0.7pt}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\;\varphi(z)\;\frac{1}{z+(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+(\mathbf{A}_{h})_{y})^{2}+\mu}

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt\times\left(-2\mathrm{i}(\mathbf{A}_{h})_{y}(x)\nabla_{x}-\mathrm{i}\divv(\mathbf{A}_{h})_{y}(x)+|(\mathbf{A}_{h})_{y}(x)|^{2}\right)\frac{1}{z+\Delta+\mu},

where
\varphi(z)=(z/\tanh(z/(2{T_{\mathrm{c}}}))-z)
. Let us define the operator
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}}^{z,V}
via the equation

\displaystyle\left(z-(K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}-V)\right)\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{z,V}=1-\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{z,V}.

(A.30)

Using (
A.28
), (
A.29
), and (
A.30
), we write the integral kernel of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}}^{z,V}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}}^{z,V}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)}\Big[\left(-2\mathrm{i}\mathbf{A}_{y}(x)\nabla_{x}-\mathrm{i}\divv\mathbf{A}_{y}(x)+|\mathbf{A}_{y}(x)|^{2}\right)\mathcal{G}^{z,V}(x,y)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt+\int_{{\hskip 0.7pt\text{\raisebox{-1.7pt}{\scalebox{1.4}{\faIconFromMacro{faVolumeOff}}}\hskip 0.7pt}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\zeta}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\;\varphi(\zeta)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}w\;\frac{1}{\zeta-(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+\mathbf{A}_{y})^{2}+\mu}(x,v)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt\times\left(-2\mathrm{i}\mathbf{A}_{y}(v)\nabla_{v}-\mathrm{i}\divv\mathbf{A}_{y}(v)+|\mathbf{A}_{y}(v)|^{2}\right)g_{0}^{\zeta}(v-w)\,\mathcal{G}^{z,V}(v,y)\Big].

(A.31)

In the next step we use this formula to prove a bound for the operator norm of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}}^{z,V}
.

Let us denote the first and the second term on the right side of (
A.31
) by
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}(x,y)
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}}^{(2)}(x,y)
, respectively. Using (
4.24
) and (
4.25
), we see that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{(1)}(x,y)|\leqslant Ch^{2}\bigl(|x-y|\,|\nabla_{x}\mathcal{G}^{z,V}(x,y)|+\bigl(|x-y|+|x-y|^{2}\bigr)|\mathcal{G}^{z,V}(x,y)|\bigr).

(A.32)

Using the resolvent identity
\mathcal{R}^{z,V}=\mathcal{R}^{z}+\mathcal{R}^{z}V\mathcal{R}^{z,V}
, we estimate the first term on the right side of (
A.32
) by

\displaystyle\left|x-y\right|\left|\nabla_{x}\mathcal{G}^{z,V}(x,y)\right|\leqslant

\displaystyle\left|x-y\right|\left|\nabla\mathcal{G}^{z}(x-y)\right|

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}w\ \left||x-w|\,\nabla\mathcal{G}^{z}(x-w)\,V(w)\,\mathcal{G}^{z,V}(w,y)\right|

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}w\ \left|\nabla\mathcal{G}^{z}(x-w)\,V(w)\,|w-y|\,\mathcal{G}^{z,V}(w,y)\right|.

(A.33)

Eq. (
A.33
) allows us to obtain the following bound for the operator norm of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}
:

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant Ch^{2}\big[\left\||\cdot|\nabla\mathcal{G}^{z}\right\|_{1}\left(1+\left\|V\right\|_{2}\|\mathcal{R}^{z,V}\|_{2;\infty}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt+\left(1+\|\nabla\mathcal{G}^{z}\|_{1}\left\|V\right\|_{2}\right)\|[x,\mathcal{R}^{z,V}]\|_{2;\infty}+\|[x,[x,\mathcal{R}^{z,V}]]\|_{2;\infty}\big].

(A.34)

From Lemma
A.3
and
A.4
, we know that there is a continuous
a\colon\rho(K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that the right side of (
A.34
) is bounded by
a(z)
. In the following we will denote by
a(z)
a generic function with these properties whose precise form may change from line to line.

To obtain a bound for the operator norm of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}}^{(2)}(z)
, we first estimate its kernel by

\displaystyle|\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{(2)}(x,y)|\leqslant Ch^{2}\Bigl(\int_{{\hskip 0.7pt\text{\raisebox{-1.7pt}{\scalebox{1.4}{\faIconFromMacro{faVolumeOff}}}\hskip 0.7pt}}}\text{d}|\zeta|\;\left|\varphi(\zeta)\right|\Bigr)\sup_{\zeta\in{\hskip 0.7pt\text{\raisebox{-1.7pt}{\scalebox{1.4}{\faIconFromMacro{faVolumeOff}}}\hskip 0.7pt}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}w\;\Bigl|\frac{1}{\zeta-(-\mathrm{i}\nabla+(\mathbf{A}_{h})_{y})^{2}+\mu}(x,v)\Bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\times\big[|v-y|\ |\nabla g_{0}^{\zeta}(v-w)|+\left(|v-y|+|v-y|^{2}\right)|g_{0}^{\zeta}(v-w)|\big]|\mathcal{G}^{z,V}(w,y)|.

(A.35)

From Lemma
4.2
we know that the absolute value of the resolvent kernel of the magnetic Laplacian is bounded from above by a function only depending on
x-v
, whose
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-norm is bounded by a constant times
f(\Re\zeta,\Im\zeta)
with
f
in (
4.4
). This, in particular, implies that this
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-norm is uniformly bounded in
\zeta\in{\hskip 0.7pt\text{\raisebox{-1.7pt}{\scalebox{1.4}{\faIconFromMacro{faVolumeOff}}}\hskip 0.7pt}}
and
h
as long as the latter is small enough, compare this to the bound in
[
16
, Eq. (6.19)]
. We use this bound,
|v-y|\leqslant|v-w|+|w-y|
, and the resolvent identity for
\mathcal{R}^{z,V}
to bound the operator norm of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}}^{(2)}
by

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{(2)}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant h^{2}C\big(\sup_{\zeta\in{\hskip 0.7pt\text{\raisebox{-1.7pt}{\scalebox{1.4}{\faIconFromMacro{faVolumeOff}}}\hskip 0.7pt}}}\|(1+|\cdot|)\nabla g_{0}^{\zeta}\|_{1}\;,\;\sup_{\zeta\in{\hskip 0.7pt\text{\raisebox{-1.7pt}{\scalebox{1.4}{\faIconFromMacro{faVolumeOff}}}\hskip 0.7pt}}}\|(1+|\cdot|^{2})g_{0}^{\zeta}\|_{1}\;,\;\|(1+|\cdot|^{2})\mathcal{G}^{z}\|_{1}\;,\;

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt\|V\|_{2}\;,\;\|\mathcal{R}^{z,V}\|_{2;\infty}\;,\;\|[x,\mathcal{R}^{z,V}]\|_{2,\infty}\;,\;\|[x,[x,\mathcal{R}^{z,V}]]\|_{2,\infty}\big).

(A.36)

The constant on the right side is an affine function of each of its arguments. From Lemma
4.4
we know that the norms involving
g_{0}^{\zeta}
are finite. In combination with Lemmas
A.3
and
A.4
, this implies that the right side of (
A.36
) is bounded by
a(z)
. We conclude that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{z,V}\|_{\infty}\leqslant a(z)\,h^{2}

(A.37)

holds.

Let
K\subset\rho(K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V)
be compact. The above bounds allow us to find
h_{0}(K)>0
such that for
z\in K
and as long as
0<h<h_{0}(K)
we can write the resolvent of
K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}-V
as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{z-(K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}-V)}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{z,V}+h^{2}\,\eta_{h}(z)\quad\text{ with }\quad\eta_{h}(z)\coloneqq h^{-2}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{z,V}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{z,V})^{n}.

(A.38)

To show that the operator norm of
(1+\pi^{2})\eta_{h}(z)
is bounded by
a(z)
, we use

\displaystyle\|(1+\pi^{2})\eta_{h}(z)\|_{\infty}\leqslant\|(1+\pi^{2})\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{z,V}\|_{\infty}\;\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}h^{2(n-1)}a(z)^{n}.

(A.39)

With the resolvent identity for
\mathcal{R}^{z,V}
and Lemma
A.3
, we easily see that the operator norm of
(1+\pi^{2})\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{z,V}
is bounded by
a(z)
. This proves the claim.
∎

With the resolvent estimates in Lemma
A.8
at hand, we turn to the proof of Proposition
A.1
. Let
\lambda<2{T_{\mathrm{c}}}
be an eigenvalue of the operator
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V
. Our assumption on
V
guarantees that it has finite multiplicity
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We choose
\varepsilon>0
such that the ball
B_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)\subseteq\mathbb{C}
contains no other point of the spectrum of
K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V
than
\lambda
and define

\displaystyle P(h)\coloneqq\int_{\partial B_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\;\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{A}}^{z,V}=\int_{\partial B_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\;\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}^{z,V}+h^{2}\int_{\partial B_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\;\eta_{h}(z).

(A.40)

From Lemma
A.8
we know that the operator norm of the second term on the right side is bounded by a constant times
h^{2}
provided
h
is small enough. The integral kernel of the first term is given by

\displaystyle\Bigl(\int_{\partial B_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\;\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}}^{z,V}\Bigr)(x,y)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(x,y)}\sum_{i=1}^{m}u_{i}(x)\overline{u_{i}(y)},

(A.41)

where the vectors
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
span the eigenspace of
\lambda
. Let us denote by
P
the projection onto that linear space. Using (
A.40
), (
A.41
), and
|\Phi_{\mathbf{A}_{h}}(x,y)|\leqslant Ch^{2}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})
, which follows from the assumption
A(0)=0
, we obtain the bound

\displaystyle\|P(h)-P\|_{\infty}\leqslant Ch^{2}\,\max_{i=1,\ldots,m}\|\,|\cdot|^{2}u_{i}\|_{2}.

(A.42)

In combination with Lemma
A.5
, this proves
\rank P(h)=m
for
h
small enough as well as the second bound in (
A.1
).

To prove the bounds for the eigenvalues we use the identity

\displaystyle(K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}-V)P(h)=\int_{\partial B_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\;z\,\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{A}}^{z,V}.

(A.43)

As long as
h
is small enough, the rank of this operator equals
m
and its eigenvalues are given by
\lambda_{1}(h),\ldots,\lambda_{m}(h)
. Similar arguments to the above for the spectral projections allow us to conclude that

\displaystyle\|(K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}-V)P(h)-(K_{T_{\mathrm{c}}}-V)P\|_{\infty}\leqslant Ch^{2}\,\max_{i=1,\ldots,m}\|\,|\cdot|^{2}u_{i}\|_{2}

(A.44)

holds. This proves the claimed bound for the eigenvalues. It remains to prove (
A.2
).

Let us write
\alpha_{*}^{h}=a(h)\alpha_{*}+b(h)\phi_{h}
with
\langle\alpha_{*},\phi_{h}\rangle=0
and
|a(h)|^{2}+|b(h)|^{2}=1
. Our assumptions imply
a(h)=\langle\alpha_{*},\alpha_{*}^{h}\rangle\geqslant 0
. We rewrite the equation
P(h)\alpha_{*}^{h}=\alpha_{*}^{h}
to see that
b(h)\phi_{h}=(P(h)-P)\alpha_{*}^{h}
. An application of (
A.1
) thus implies
|b(h)|\leqslant Ch^{2}
. Using this,
|a(h)|^{2}+|b(h)|^{2}=1
, and the fact that
a(h)\geqslant 0
, we see that
|a(h)-1|\leqslant Ch^{2}
. This allows us to conclude that

\displaystyle\|(1+\pi^{2})(\alpha_{*}^{h}-\alpha_{*})\|_{2}\leqslant|a(h)-1|\ \|(1+\pi^{2})\alpha_{*}\|_{2}+|b(h)|\ \|(1+\pi^{2})\phi_{h}\|_{2}\leqslant Ch^{2}.

(A.45)

To obtain the result, we used Lemma
A.5
to see that
\|(1+\pi^{2})\alpha_{*}\|_{2}<\infty
, as well as
\|(1+\pi^{2})\phi_{h}\|_{2}\leqslant\|(1+\pi^{2})\alpha_{*}\|_{2}+\|(1+\pi^{2})\alpha_{*}^{h}\|_{2}
, and

\displaystyle\|(1+\pi^{2})\alpha_{*}^{h}\|_{2}\leqslant C\|K_{{T_{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{A}}\alpha_{*}^{h}\|_{2}\leqslant C\left(|\lambda(h)|+\|V\alpha_{*}^{h}\|_{2}\right)\leqslant C\left(|\lambda(h)|+\|V\|_{\infty}\right).

(A.46)

This proves (
A.2
) and also finishes the proof of Proposition
A.1
.
